                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                  Author: Branislav Stanicek
                                 Members' Research Service
                                 PE 762.434 – October 2024                                             EN
                                                        78
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Two and a half years into the war in Ukraine, the EU and its partners continue to demonstrate their
unity, resilience and capacity to develop what Ursula von der Leyen defined as a 'geopolitical
Commission', which accelerates enlargement and stabilises its neighbourhood. The accession
process and the response to the war against Ukraine will determine the EU's geopolitical status in
the coming years, and the nature of its international relations.
The Granada Declaration adopted by EU Heads of State or Government in October 2023 stressed
that 'Enlargement is a geo-strategic investment in peace, security, stability and prosperity'. The
strategic dimension of the enlargement policy became more pronounced in 2022, when Ukraine,
Moldova and Georgia requested to join the EU. Since then, fresh challenges have accelerated
reforms of the enlargement policy, including the implementation of a new accession negotiations
methodology, and cemented a shift towards a more political process based on merit-based
integration. Strategic considerations include security and defence, as well as foreign policy
alignment of candidate countries, for instance in terms of relations with strategic competitors or
rivals, such as China and Russia.
The main tasks of the Enlargement portfolio as set out in the mission letter from the Commission
president-elect to Kos consists of driving enlargement momentum built upon recent reforms. This
involves overseeing the accession negotiations process with candidate countries, including
maintaining close relations with their political representatives, in order to ensure greater political
commitments and support for the candidate countries' reform agenda. Enlargement also presents
challenges for other policy areas, such as the EU's institutional decision-making and budget, and
reconstruction of Ukraine with the cooperation of international donors and organisations, such as
the International Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank and G7 members. Security and defence, as well
as regional cooperation and the resolution of bilateral and territorial disputes meanwhile remain key
elements of Kos's portfolio.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
According to Eurobarometer surveys from April 2024, the majority of EU citizens polled would like to see
increased EU involvement in all policy areas relating to foreign affairs, peace and security. The surveys depict
citizens' continued attachment to peace and democratic values. Looking to the future, EU citizens rank defence
and security (37 %) as one of the top priorities in reinforcing the EU's global position, followed by energy issues
and food security/agriculture (both at 30 %). While 40 % of citizens believe the EU's role in the world has
become more important, others believe it has stayed the same (35 %) or that it has diminished (22 %).
Furthermore, 51 % of respondents support EU enlargement in the future. Some 79 % of respondents see
Russia's invasion of Ukraine as a threat to EU security, and 75 % believe that by standing against Russia's
invasion of Ukraine, the EU is defending EU values. This is despite the fact that 81 % of respondents believe
the war in Ukraine has serious financial consequences for their country. In this context, 70 % agree with the
financial support given to Ukraine and 60 % support granting Ukraine candidate country status.

Making the EU institutions enlargement-ready
The Commission communication on pre-accession reforms and policy reviews of March 2024
stresses that EU institutional reforms 'become indispensable' with enlargement. The report of the
Franco-German working group on EU institutional reform proposes policies and reforms to ensure
that the EU institutions are 'enlargement-ready'. Furthermore, future enlargement could spark a
debate on whether to move away from unanimity in the Council towards more majority voting
(including by rebalancing the qualified majority threshold to benefit smaller EU countries), as
suggested in a 2023 EPRS cost of non-Europe paper. The gradual, merit-based integration of
candidate countries would also require increased EU expenditure and a budget that is more strictly
conditional on rule of law-related reforms.



2
                                                      79
                                                                                                 Enlargement


Budgetary challenges
The accession of new countries suggests that the EU budget will need to grow over the coming
budgetary period. The mid-term review of the MFF in 2024 already revealed the extent to which the
2021-2027 budget is stretched beyond its limit, proposing additional contributions from Member
States. A 2023 Brueghel report suggests that the pandemic and the ensuing recovery instruments
financed through the issuance of EU debt have created additional challenges in terms of increased
borrowing costs, as rising interest costs will place additional constraints on the EU budget after 2027
when post-pandemic EU debt reimbursement begins. These challenges augur potential risks in light
of enlargement, Ukraine macro-financial assistance (MFA) and reconstruction programmes, as both
the MFA programmes and the Ukraine Facility – including the most recent €35 billion MFA – are
financed through new EU debt issuance.

Support for Ukraine and reconstruction costs
The third Rapid Damage and Needs Assessment (RDNA3) report, released by the Commission in
February 2024, estimated that reconstruction and recovery in Ukraine would cost a total of
€452.8 billion over the next decade. So far, the EU and its Member States have supported Ukraine
with €118 billion. Agreed funds include the Ukraine Facility – the EU's flagship programme to
support Ukraine's recovery, reconstruction and modernisation – which mobilises €50 billion to
ensure support over the 2024-2027 period. Of this amount, €13.8 billion has already been disbursed,
leaving €36.2 billion available. New extraordinary revenues generated by EU operators were agreed
upon in principle on 14 June 2024, at the G7 Apulia meeting, to make an additional US$50 billion in
funding available by Euroclear until the end of 2024. However, as no consensus was reached among
G7 states regarding the implementation of the commitments made in Apulia, the EU announced its
decision to move forward alone with a loan to Ukraine of €35 billion secured by revenues sourced
from frozen Russian assets.

Security and defence
Once part of the EU, every Member State would benefit from the EU's mutual defence clause, as set
out in Article 42(7) of the Treaty on European Union (TEU). This commitment binds EU countries to
aid fellow Member States that fall victim to armed aggression 'by all the means in their power'. Some
analysts suggest that Article 42(7) TEU carries a higher degree of legal obligation than the
Washington Treaty (NATO) equivalent, Article 5. The latter states that each party has an obligation
of mutual defence 'as it deems necessary, including the use of armed force'. However, it is not clear
how the activation of Article 42(7) TEU would work in practice. Currently, to address Ukraine's needs
in the event of aggression, the joint security commitments between the EU and Ukraine, signed on
27 June 2024, include the activation of an extraordinary consultations mechanism within 24 hours.
Finally, territorial disputes and frozen conflicts in Transnistria (Moldova), Abkhazia and South
Ossetia (Georgia) will no doubt weigh on those countries' efforts to join the EU.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Parliament's powers and influence in the context of enlargement are considerable. Under Article 49 TEU and
Article 218 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the EU (TFEU), its consent is required for any accession to the
EU. The EU Treaties mandate that Parliament be immediately and fully informed at all stages of international
negotiation processes, including trade negotiations (Article 207(3) TFEU). Parliament not only has a veto
power over association and cooperation agreements but also over financial protocols and instruments with
third countries. Moreover, the Lisbon Treaty states that the Parliament must be consulted on the main aspects
and choices of the common foreign and security policy (CFSP). Twice a year, it must hold a debate on the
progress in implementing the CFSP, including the common security and defence policy (CSDP) (Article 36
TEU). Parliament serves as a co-legislator on financial instruments of foreign policy.




                                                                                                             3
                                                    80
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Recent developments
In February 2020, the Commission adopted a revised methodology for accession negotiations,
marked by setting up negotiation clusters and providing higher political steer to the enlargement
process, by organising annual EU-Western Balkans summits. In June 2022, European Council
conclusions retained a gradual approach to accession, meaning acceding countries would be able to
enjoy some of the EU single market's benefits even before their official accession. In
November 2023, the Commission adopted a new growth plan for the Western Balkans, which
includes a proposal for a €6 billion reform and growth facility for the region for 2024-2027, adopted
by the Council in May 2024.
In the Western Balkans, accession negotiations with Montenegro and Serbia are well under way.
While the EU agreed in principle to open negotiations with Albania and North Macedonia in 2022, on
25 September 2024, EU ambassadors agreed to start negotiations with Albania on 15 October, while
postponing the decision on North Macedonia. In March 2024, the European Council decided to open
accession negotiations with Bosnia and Herzegovina. Subsequently, the most critical task in the
region is to complete the comprehensive agreement within the Belgrade-Pristina dialogue, which
held its 11th high level meeting in Brussels on 26 June 2024. Türkiye, still a candidate country,
remains a 'key partner' eligible for EU financial support. However, falling democratic standards and
a very low alignment rate of 10 % with the EU stance on foreign and security policy (2023), compared
with 8 % in 2022, have put accession negotiations on ice.
On 28 February 2022, Volodymyr Zelenskyy announced his country's request for 'immediate
accession via a new special procedure'. The move was followed by both Moldova and Georgia.
Considering the Commission's positive opinions, on 23 June 2022, EU leaders decided to grant
Ukraine and Moldova 'candidate country' status. While Georgia received 'candidate country' status
in December 2023, its recent backsliding has de facto led to a halt in the accession process. In the
case of Ukraine and Moldova, the first intergovernmental conferences took place on 25 June 2024,
officially kicking off accession negotiations.
FURTHER READING
Bassot E., The six policy priorities of the von der Leyen Commission: An end-of-term assessment, EPRS,
European Parliament, April 2024.
European Parliament, Marta Kos hearing documents and CV, 2024.
Stanicek B. and Leclerc G., EU-Western Balkans relations: Macroeconomic situation and EU financial
support, EPRS, European Parliament, April 2024.
Stanicek B. and Przetacznik J., Enlargement policy: Reforms and challenges ahead, EPRS, European
Parliament, December 2023.

BRIEFING
Confirmation hearings of the
Commissioners-designate


                                  Jozef Síkela
                         International Partnerships
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Wednesday 6 November, at 14.30.
European Parliament committee responsible: Development (DEVE)

Jozef Síkela (Mayors and Independents Party, STAN) affiliated to the European People's Party (EPP),
has served as the Czech minister for industry and trade since December 2021. In this position, his
focus has been on reducing his country's reliance on Russian gas, developing the use of renewable
energy sources and securing stakes in German and Dutch liquefied natural gas (LNG).
Síkela has served in various banks, notably as the head of the Slovak Savings Bank and as board
member of the Austrian Erste Group Bank.
Born in 1967 in Rokycany, Czechia, Síkela studied foreign trade economics at the Prague University
of Economics and Business.



                                          IN THIS BRIEFING
                                              Fundamentals of the portfolio
                                              Priorities and challenges
                                              European Parliament
                                              Recent developments




Jozef Síkela, Czechia.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                             Author: Eric Pichon and Marc Jutten
                                 Members' Research Service
                                PE 762.439 – October 2024                                              EN
                                                      82
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
The rise of new geopolitical powers, such as China, Russia, India and Turkey, has caused a shift in
the EU's key partner role in third countries. In 2019, this prompted a rebranding of the Development
Cooperation Commissioner's portfolio to 'International Partnerships'. This reflects the EU's ambition
to form balanced relationships, no longer replicating the donor-recipient model with conditionalities
but instead resting on mutually beneficial agreements in areas such as critical raw materials,
infrastructure, and migration management. The EU's partnerships with lower-income countries,
especially the least developed ones, are guided by the 2017 European consensus on development –
a holistic doctrine aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda and Sustainable Development Goals
(SDGs). While retaining poverty eradication as the main goal of EU development policy, the
consensus underlines that addressing the root causes of migration is pivotal to achieving the overall
objectives of EU external action. It also reaffirms the EU's commitment to rights-based principles
and values, with a specific focus on gender equality.
In her mission letter to Síkela, Commission President-elect Ursula von der Leyen named work on the
Global Gateway strategy as a key priority. Launched in 2021, this strategy is designed to boost
smart, clean and secure connections in the digital, energy and transport sectors, and to strengthen
health, education and research systems globally. In this context, Síkela will have to work towards
mobilising investment by bringing together the efforts of the EU, its Member States and their
financial and development institutions. Síkela's portfolio responsibilities may require that he
cooperate with the Commissioner for the Mediterranean, for external aspects of migration, and the
Commissioner in charge of humanitarian aid, to improve delivery on short- and longer-term needs.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In a September 2023 Eurobarometer survey, 75 % of the EU citizens polled supported the idea of the EU
engaging with partner countries. Above all, they expressed support for EU cooperation with third countries
on peace and security (40 %). Three quarters felt that tackling poverty in third countries should be a major EU'
priority, but only a quarter considered the EU successful in this area. The EU is perceived to have had more
success in improving transport infrastructure (69 %) and digital connectivity (67 %) around the world.

Priority partnerships
To counterbalance the growing influence of new players, Síkela is tasked with deepening
cooperation with like-minded partners. On partnerships with countries from the African continent,
the implementation of the 2020 EU-Africa strategy has been hindered by differing views on Russia's
invasion of Ukraine, a perceived double standard in EU migration and asylum policies, and the impact
of the support for Ukraine on the EU's contribution to African peace operations. Following coups in
the Sahel and West Africa, the EU is reassessing its strategic presence in the region. The aim is also
to increase involvement in the Indo-Pacific. This includes a new partnership with India and stronger
ties with Japan, South Korea, Australia and New Zealand. EU engagement with Latin American and
Caribbean countries should also be strengthened, following up on commitments made during the
2023 summit of the EU and the Community of Latin American and Caribbean States.
Under the Samoa Agreement, the EU aims to simplify its relations with sub-Saharan African,
Caribbean, and Pacific countries, but is challenged by overlap with other frameworks with the
African Union and other regional groupings and concerns about implementation challenges.

Securing the EU's economic needs while ensuring mutual interest
By 2050, it is estimated that four of the world's five largest economies (China, India, Indonesia and
Brazil) will come from the Global South. In this context, to foster the EU's international influence,
plans under the Global Gateway strategy include mobilising investment of up to €300 billion



2
                                                     83
                                                                                   International Partnerships


between 2021 and 2027. Experts are calling for clearer Global Gateway governance and strategies.
Síkela will need to align the Global Gateway with the EU's economic objectives of competitiveness and
sustainability in the areas of green energy, critical raw materials, connectivity, health and education.
The Critical Raw Materials (CRMs) Act seeks to secure a steady supply of CRMs to secure EU
autonomy in its green transition while promoting value-added activities in partner countries.
Strategic partnerships, for instance with Chile or Mongolia, will involve not only Síkela but also the
Commissioners responsible for trade and competitiveness. Here, Síkela must demonstrate EU
commitment to relationships of mutual interest with partner countries, to forge lasting alliances at
multilateral level. The policy coherence for development (PCD) concept must be made a reality.
PCD requires that the EU take into account the SDGs and the goal of poverty eradication in all
policies likely to affect developing countries. This poses challenges: the EU's demand for CRMs and
hydrogen may boost economies but harm the environment and workers' conditions in partner
countries. Regulations on deforestation and carbon tax, aimed at achieving sustainability, might
harm vulnerable economies unable to adapt. The EU migration policy's increased focus on security,
labour shortages, and readmission could lead to a misalignment with partner countries' interests.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
EU development policy is a shared competence of the EU and the Member States. Parliament co-legislates
with the Council. Parliament has a right of scrutiny over implementation of the policy (Articles 208 to 212 of
the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union).

Parliament strongly supports the policy coherence for development principle and calls for an EU
action plan to help mainstream development considerations in all EU policies from the outset. In
theprevious term, Parliament had a standing rapporteur on PCD and a PCD network composed of
representatives from several committees and delegations. Parliament also calls for the EU to use aid
more effectively and efficiently by linking humanitarian, development, migration and security
interventions and by strengthening joint EU and Member State programming.
Parliament holds observer status on the Global Gateway Board. In the framework of the future
European financial architecture for development, Parliament calls for effective governance of the
Global Gateway and demands to be involved in decisions on relevant investments.
Parliament has emphasised the critical importance of addressing food insecurity and biodiversity
loss in developing countries. This entails supporting research on, and preserving, local plant genetic
resources and diets, promoting sustainable farming and fishing, evaluating trade deals'
environmental impact, and combating wildlife trafficking and illegal logging.
Parliament has called for the EU-Africa strategy to prioritise human development and human rights
and involve civil society, local authorities and national parliaments. It calls for closer cooperation in
key areas such as security, agriculture and health. It suggests that the renewed partnership should
help tackle the root causes of migration and deliver an effective legal migration policy.
Parliament approved the Samoa Agreement on 10 April 2024, nevertheless expressing concerns
about the agreement's weak provisions on civil society involvement and discrimination based on
gender or sexual identity. It hoped that the agreement could scale up security cooperation.

Recent developments
Team Europe and the Global Gateway
Official development assistance (ODA) remains crucial for poorer economies, with the EU and its
Member States providing 42 % of global ODA. However, ODA alone is insufficient to address all the
challenges faced by developing countries. To complement their efforts, the EU and its Member
States have launched Team Europe initiatives, among them the above-mentioned Global Gateway,



                                                                                                            3
                                                    84
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


to leverage private investment. Team Europe is composed of the EU and its Member States —
including their implementing agencies and public development banks — as well as the European
Investment Bank (EIB), the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD) and the
private sector. The 'team' seeks to mobilise up to €300 billion in investment for sustainable and high-
quality projects in partner countries by 2027, to narrow the global investment gap.

EU with Africa strategy and Samoa Agreement with ACP states
In June 2020, the Council approved the EU with Africa strategy, focusing on the green transition,
digitalisation, economic growth, peace, governance, and migration. The strategy also placed
emphasis on strengthened EU-Africa collaboration on the global stage. In November 2022, the
African Union (AU) Commission and the European Commission took stock of progress and launched
a €750 million programme in support of infrastructure investment in Africa.
Provisional application of the Samoa Agreement with members of the Organisation of African,
Caribbean, and Pacific States (OACPS) began on 1 January 2024. The agreement is based on six key
priorities: human rights, democracy and governance; peace and security; human and social
development; inclusive, sustainable economic growth and development; environmental
sustainability and climate change; and migration and mobility. It will enter fully into force after each
EU Member State and at least two thirds of OACPS members have ratified it.

Migration partnerships
The Parliament and Council adopted the new pact on migration and asylum in the spring of 2024.
The external policy component emphasises the need for collaboration with countries of migration
origin and transit. It seeks to build partnerships that include provisions on limiting entry and
facilitating the readmission of illegal migrants or asylum-seekers. However, several countries are
reluctant to engage in migration partnerships that could hinder transnational or circular migration or
reduce remittance flows without improving access to legal migration pathways. The Commission
proposed a tougher approach towards third countries' failure to cooperate on readmission, by
restricting visa delivery or preferential access to the EU market. However, Parliament's INTA
committee rejected this proposal, deeming it incompatible with the SDGs and fundamental rights.

European Parliament, 2024.
Committee on Development, 2019-2024 activity report, European Parliament, April 2024.

Commissioners-designate


                                  Costas Kadis
                             Fisheries and Oceans
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Wednesday 6 November, at 14.30.
European Parliament committee responsible: Fisheries (PECH)

Costas (Costantinos) Kadis is a scientist with a solid academic profile who has held ministerial
portfolios in health, education and agriculture in various Cypriot governments.
Kadis is professor of biodiversity conservation at Frederick University, where he also heads the
Nature Conservation Unit and has been appointed as deputy president of the University Council.
Between 2014 and 2023, Kadis served in two successive Cypriot governments without belonging to
a political party. He was minister for education and culture (2014-2018), and minister for agriculture,
rural development and environment (2018-2023). In the latter position, he was also responsible for
fisheries and marine research.
Born in Nicosia in 1967, Kadis graduated and obtained a PhD in biology from the University of Athens.
After several research-related positions in Greece and Cyprus, he joined Frederick University in
Cyprus in 2005, where he was appointed associate professor, founded the Nature Conservation Unit,
and was Dean of the School of Education. He held these positions until 2014, apart from the period
from July 2007 to February 2008 when he served as minister of health (as a member of the centre-
right Democratic Party (DIKO). Kadis also chaired the Greek Cypriot Environment Committee from
2008 to 2014 as part of the government's (unsuccessful) efforts to reunify Cyprus. This work did
lead to important environmental cooperation agreements with the Turkish Cypriot community.




                                                 IN THIS BRIEFING
                                                       Fundamentals of the portfolio
                                                       Priorities and challenges
                                                       European Parliament
                                                       Recent developments




Costas Kadis, Cyprus.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:
https://epthinktank.eu/commissioner_hearings_2024.
                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                  Author: Frederik Scholaert
                                  Members' Research Service
                                  PE 762.435 – October 2024                                               EN
                                                  86
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
As Commissioner for Fisheries and Oceans, Costas Kadis will work under the guidance of the
Vice-President for Cohesion and Reforms and will be responsible for the Directorate-General for
Maritime Affairs and Fisheries (DG MARE). The main policy in his portfolio is common fisheries
policy (CFP), last reformed in 2013 to ensure that fisheries and aquaculture are sustainable
environmentally, economically and socially. Now, a decade after this reform took place, the policy is
being evaluated. In her mission letter to Costas Kadis, European Commission President-elect Ursula
von der Leyen requests that he complete the CFP evaluation. She emphasises the importance of a
level playing-field for EU operators, such as protecting them from unfair competition and ensuring
their access to neighbouring waters. A European oceans pact is necessary to ensure coherence
between all ocean-related policies.
The 2013 CFP reform focused on sustainable exploitation through a scientific parameter called the
maximum sustainable yield (MSY). Tools to support this aim include multiannual plans (MAPs) by
sea basin, 'regionalised decision-making', and the landing obligation (to end discarding of unwanted
catches). The European Maritime, Fisheries and Aquaculture Fund (EMFAF), is the main fund
dedicated to the CFP. It provides aid to the sector, while also promoting the blue economy and
contributing to marine protection and ocean governance.
In addition to the CFP, DG MARE manages other policies such as the blue economy agenda (which
aims to promote sustainability, skills, innovation and synergies), maritime spatial planning
(optimising the use of maritime space), the algae initiative (which aims to unlock the EU's
bio-economy potential in seaweed) and the international ocean governance agenda.
Other ocean-related policies are managed by other DGs and will therefore require coordination.
Examples include the nature restoration law (aiming to restore 20 % of the EU's marine area by
2030), the offshore renewable energy strategy (setting long-term targets by sea basin), the Marine
Strategy Framework Directive (the MSFD, aiming to achieve 'good environmental status' in marine
waters), and the 'Restore our Ocean and Waters' mission under the EU's research programme.

Priorities and challenges
In 2023, the Commission published a fisheries and oceans package. Two implementation reports
highlighted the positive results achieved since the 2013 reform. The Commission therefore did not
propose a new reform but instead focused on improving the implementation of the CFP. Recent
challenges for the sector include attracting young people and introducing innovation.
Furthermore, two action plans on energy and marine protection have sparked much debate.
Energy is a major cost item for fisheries due to the sector's heavy reliance on fossil fuels. The recent
crisis, sparked by Russia's invasion of Ukraine, has even threatened its profitability. To address this
issue and link it to the goals of the Green Deal, the Commission published the energy action plan,
which aims to increase energy efficiency and switch to renewable and low-carbon energy sources.
Two barriers to this transition are funding (the EMFAF is a small fund) and the fleet capacity ceilings
under the CFP (the weight of a vessel is part of the definition of capacity; as new technologies
typically require more weight than conventional diesel engines, this could lead to an increase in
vessel capacity and exceed a ceiling). Overcoming these barriers will be challenging.
The marine action plan seeks to enhance the coordinated implementation of environmental and
fisheries policies. In particular, it aims to make fishing practices more sustainable by improving
fishing gear and reducing the impact on vulnerable species and the seabed. Highly controversial is
the Commission’s call for a ban on mobile bottom fishing gear in all marine protected areas (MPAs).
A key legislative initiative will focus on the establishment of the EMFAF for the 2028-2034 period.
The current EMFAF allows aid, such as engine modernisation, for vessels up to 24 metres in length.
In 2022, a World Trade Organization (WTO) agreement was reached to ban certain fisheries




2
                                                     87
                                                                                        Fisheries and Oceans


subsidies, including for fisheries targeting overfished stocks. This agreement was ratified by the EU
in 2023. It remains to be seen whether it will have an impact on the EU's support for fishing vessels.
The Commission reports on the state of play in each sea basin in its annual communication on EU
fisheries. In the Atlantic, average exploitation is now in line with the MSY. However, a challenge
arises in the relationship with northern third countries. The EU-UK Trade and Cooperation
Agreement (TCA) guarantees mutual access to waters until mid-2026, after which decisions on
mutual access will be taken as part of the annual negotiations on fishing opportunities. It is important
to note that fisheries measures should not discriminate against vessels from either party, though
there have been disputes over recent UK measures. Relations with other northern countries have
also become more complex. For example, the EU and Norway have disagreements over Svalbard
cod quotas post-Brexit. Costas Kadis will need to ensure good cooperation in all northern
agreements to navigate these challenges.
The Baltic Sea is in a dire state, mainly due to pollution (such as eutrophication from nutrient inputs)
and climate change, rather than overfishing. The second Our Baltic conference, hosted in 2023 by
the Commissioner for Environment, Oceans and Fisheries, assessed the progress made. Since the
environment is no longer in this portfolio, cooperation with the Commissioner responsible will be
needed. In fisheries, the ecosystem-based approach must be fully implemented. For 2025, further
catch reductions have been proposed for most Baltic stocks in accordance with science and the
Baltic MAP. Other (future) measures may include spatial or temporal fisheries closures, measures to
reduce by-catch, or more tailored solutions (e.g. the Commission has mentioned the use of mussels
to reverse eutrophication). Specific aid for cessation of fishing activities may be necessary (as has
been decided for Baltic cod and western herring fisheries). A recent report on the Baltic MAP viewed
it as a valuable tool, providing a safety net for stocks in difficulty and flexibility for healthy stocks.
In the Mediterranean, overfishing remains a challenge, although some progress has been made
(between 2007 and 2022, overfishing dropped from 90 % to 20 % above MSY). The EU has adopted
a western Mediterranean MAP for demersal fisheries, with an implementation report published in
July 2024. Although it led to positive results, an NGO found a high risk of missing the 2025 MSY
target. Since many other stocks are shared with third countries, Mediterranean and Black Sea
countries work together in the General Fisheries Commission for the Mediterranean (GFCM). For
example, the GFCM has adopted 11 multiannual plans. As a member, the EU wields significant
influence in shaping its decisions and must ensure that they are transposed into EU law (a new
proposal has been tabled to this end). The Commission recently proposed the 2025 fishing
opportunities (the allowed catches and fishing days) for the Mediterranean and Black Seas.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
According to Articles 3 and 4 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU), the marine
fisheries sector is one of the few exclusive EU competences, while for other areas of the CFP (such as
aquaculture and seafood processing), the EU shares competence with Member States. According to Article 43
Parliament and the Council must act in accordance with the ordinary legislative procedure when it comes to
fisheries policy, with the exception of the setting of fishing opportunities (where only the Council decides).
Under Article 218, international fisheries agreements are concluded by the Council after obtaining consent
from Parliament.

The EU's exclusive competence for marine fisheries, combined with the fact that, since the Lisbon
Treaty, the Parliament co-legislates most fisheries legislation on an equal footing with the Council,
makes the Parliament an important player in fisheries policy.
During its previous term, the Parliament outlined its vision for fisheries and ocean policies in a
number of resolutions. Parliament's January 2024 resolution on the state of play of the CFP, served
as its contribution to the ongoing evaluation. The Parliament emphasised the need for continued
CFP implementation but expressed regret that socio-economic aspects had not been sufficiently



                                                                                                            3
                                                    88
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


taken into account. It also reiterated its stance on the landing obligation, namely that it should be
reviewed, in particular to limit the problem of 'choke species' (i.e. species for which, once the quota
is reached, fishers may need to stop fishing entirely to avoid the risk of by-catching them while
targeting other species). The resolution also highlighted that MSY is 'impossible to apply' in mixed
fisheries, where multiple species are targeted simultaneously. Parliament called for multiannual
TACs, to give more certainty to fishers. It also reiterated its view on the need for a broader definition
of small-scale fisheries beyond just vessel length. On capacity ceilings as an obstacle to the energy
transition, Parliament recommended redefining capacity, for example, by excluding the tonnage
related to 'social and safety' from the calculation of capacity.
In a resolution on the marine action plan, Parliament criticised the Commission for its simplistic
approach when proposing to ban mobile bottom fishing in all MPAs by 2030. Parliament called for
the involvement of fishers in the implementation of the MPAs. The plan should also prevent conflicts
in light of the growing use of maritime space, such as for offshore wind energy.

Recent developments
As a follow-up to the fisheries and oceans package and the feedback received, the Commission has
launched a new consultation on the CFP. The results will be presented in 2025. Two other
consultations, on the current EMFAF for 2021-2027 and the previous fund (for 2014-2020), have just
closed and will feed into a mid-term evaluation by the end of 2024. Several initiatives have been
launched following the energy action plan, with the latest being a call for an advisory support group.
In Parliament, an overview of the activities of the Committee on Fisheries (PECH) for 2019-2024 has
been published (among these, the EMFAF and the fisheries control system were two important
legislative files). More recently, a study on recent developments and future challenges in the EU's
oceans and fisheries policy was presented to PECH on 23 September 2024. It includes policy
recommendations on fisheries, aquaculture, the blue economy and ocean governance.
Furthermore, as regards ocean governance, the EU announced, as it does every year, a list of
commitments (this year worth €3.5 billion) at the Our Ocean conference in Greece in April 2024.
FURTHER READING
Cappell R. et al., The EU oceans and fisheries policy - Latest developments and future challenges, Policy
Department for Structural and Cohesion Policies, European Parliament, September 2024.
Committee on Fisheries, 2019-2024 activity report, European Parliament, April 2024.
European Parliament, Constantinos Kadis hearing documents and CV, 2024.
European Commission, Oceans and fisheries (website).
Scholaert F., Common fisheries policy: State of play, EPRS, European Parliament, October 2023.

Confirmation hearing due to be held on Wednesday 6 November, at 9.00.
European Parliament committee responsible: Economic and Monetary Affairs (ECON)

and Nomination committees at Morgan Stanley, and a member of the Operating Team at Horizon
Equity Partners. She was a member of the ISCTE University Institute of Lisbon's Executive Education
Advisory Board.

(2019-2020).
In her political career, she has served as an elected member of the Municipal Assembly of Almada

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                         Authors: Issam Hallak and Stefano Spinaci
                               Members' Research Service
                               PE 762.430 – October 2024                                               EN
                                                     90
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
The European Commission President-elect, Ursula von der Leyen, has identified developing the
European savings and investments union as fundamental for the new Commission, as it would bring
the ambition of a single market for financial services much closer to fruition. Once in place, this union
is expected to leverage substantial amounts of private capital to finance the green, digital and social
transitions. This includes the completion of the banking union and the capital markets union (CMU),
both launched following the global financial crisis. The EU has completed the single supervision and
single resolution mechanisms of the banking union – known as Pillar 1 and Pillar 2 – leaving the
European deposit insurance scheme (EDIS) – Pillar 3 – at the proposal stage. The CMU is a relatively
more complex policy plan involving actions in a wide range of areas, such as securities markets,
securitisation, insurance companies and business insolvency law. EU-level policy actions consist
mainly of harmonisation of rules to overcome cross-border investment hurdles, and while major
steps have been made in this area, more actions are yet to come.
The portfolio also includes financial sector-related components that require coordination among the
Member States. For instance, the action plan on sustainable finance presented by the European
Commission in 2018 sought to re-orient capital flows towards sustainable investment; to manage
financial risks due to environmental, social and governance (ESG) risks; and to foster transparency
and long-termism. Finally, the portfolio includes measures to enhance retail investment through
protection and financial literacy of EU citizens as a way to ensure that they have the knowledge and
skills to optimise their savings and investment management. Achieving success in this domain will
favour the retail investment enhancement policy.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
A 2022 Eurobarometer survey on retail financial services and products showed that EU citizens care about
sustainable finance. More than six in ten respondents did not want their savings and investments to fund
economic activities that have a negative impact on the planet. About half of respondents agreed that they
were more likely to invest in a financial product if they knew it was sustainable. Moreover, about one in five
respondents felt that their rights had been breached when opening a bank account, making payments, taking
out a consumer loan or a loan to buy a home, buying an insurance policy or taking out an investment product.

In her mission letter to Albuquerque, von der Leyen identifies unlocking the financing needed for the
green, digital and social transitions as a core priority. Policy actions include designing EU-level
savings and investment products, working on leveraging the potential of private and occupational
pensions, reviewing the regulatory framework to ensure financing for fast-growing innovative start-
ups, increasing sustainable finance, improving capital markets supervision, and developing a
strategy on financial literacy.
The CMU action plan proposed in 2020 includes several ongoing legislative procedures. Among
these, interinstitutional negotiations on the retail investor protection package are set to begin.
Ensuring that retail investors further access capital markets thanks to enhanced protection and
financial literacy will be among Albuquerque's priorities.
Another major priority will be to support the private financing of fast-growing innovation companies.
This has also been identified as a serious issue by the reports on the future of the single market, by
former Italian Prime Minister, Enrico Letta, and on EU competitiveness, by former European Central
Bank President, Mario Draghi.
However, there are considerable challenges inherent in the harmonisation of financial regulations,
which is at the heart of the CMU. These include the fact that Member States have different legal
frameworks and cultural backgrounds, and that putting the CMU in place may mean hiring new staff
and developing new competences. One priority is thus to ensure that EU laws are adequately
enforced and that the supervisory system at EU level is streamlined.



2
                                                     91
                                                   Financial Services and the Savings and Investments Union


European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Chapter 4 Article 63 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the EU (TFEU) prohibits all restrictions on the
movement of capital between EU Member States, unless they are necessary for legitimate public interests.
However, significant technical barriers have remained, and new legislation has been adopted to remove them.
The legal basis for policy actions mainly relies on two TFEU articles. The first, Article 114 TFEU on the
approximation of law, is the most frequently used. It allows for the adoption of measures to align provisions
set by law in EU Member States, which have as their goal the establishment and functioning of the internal
market (Article 26 TFEU). The second, Article 53 TFEU, provides that, in order to facilitate individuals in
starting and pursuing activities as self-employed, the co-legislators have to issue directives for the mutual
recognition of formal qualifications and the coordination of provisions laid down by law in Member States for
the activities of the self-employed.

During its last term, the European Parliament consistently supported and voted in favour of
completing the banking union and the CMU, highlighting their importance for achieving the EU's
strategic goals. In its 2021 report on strengthening the international role of the euro, Parliament
emphasised the need to deepen and complete European monetary union, the banking union and the
CMU, to boost EU markets' international competitiveness, enhance the stability and attractiveness
of the euro and, ultimately, strengthen the EU's strategic autonomy.
In its latest report on banking union, adopted in January 2024, Parliament noted that the banking
union was an 'essential complement' to European monetary union and the single market. Parliament
also urged the European Commission to prioritise the completion of the banking union and the CMU
in its upcoming term. In its September 2020 report on further development of the capital markets
union, Parliament listed five areas for action, with the promotion of long-term and cross-border
investment identified as a top priority. Parliament also recommended gradually granting the
European securities and markets authority (ESMA) direct supervisory powers to improve efficiency,
and advocated the establishment of a single EU supervisory authority.

Recent developments
In the previous term, the European Commission initiated significant action in four different areas:
CMU, digital finance, sustainable finance and banking, with corresponding action plans. One major
action plan, from 2020, focused on preparations for establishing the CMU. Key completed actions
under this plan include the adoption of the Listing Act – expected to facilitate the issuance of market
securities by medium-sized firms – and the establishment of the European single access point and
the consolidated price quotations – known as consolidated tape – to improve the flow of information
within the EU.
In the digital finance package, tabled by the Commission in 2020, the Markets in Crypto-Assets
Regulation (MICAR) was finalised and represents a major step toward a unified regulatory
framework for unregulated assets utilising distributed ledger technologies. MiCAR specifically
focuses on regulating crypto-assets with a stable value tied to official currencies such as the euro,
known as stablecoin. Risks posed by the rapid expansion of information and communication
technology (ICT) will be monitored through the adopted Regulation on Digital Operational
Resilience. Additionally, the digital finance package includes proposals addressing payment services
and open finance.
The package for financial data access and payments comprises three proposals that cover data
access and payment services. The first proposal on financial data access seeks to establish a
framework for accessing individual and business customer data across a wide range of financial
services. The other two proposals centre on amending the payment services regime and establishing
a payment services regulation, respectively. Finally, the Commission tabled proposals to introduce
a digital euro that would complement the physical euro by offering it in digital form.



                                                                                                           3
                                                    92
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


A number of legislative acts were implemented in the area of sustainable finance. The Taxonomy
Regulation provides a classification system for sustainable activities; the EU Green Bond Standard
Regulation sets a uniform definition of green bonds; the Sustainable Finance Disclosure Regulation
clarifies the duties of asset managers and institutional investors duties regarding sustainability; and
the ESG Rating Regulation improves the reliability and transparency of ESG rating activities.
Additionally, sustainability reporting was reinforced through the Corporate Sustainability Reporting
Directive and the Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive, both designed to provide more
transparency and accountability on sustainability issues related to economic activities that are
especially targeted at financial investors.
In banking, the EU adopted new rules on the minimum capital requirement levels applicable to all
banks in line with the finalisation of international agreements known as Basel III. The Commission
also introduced a proposal to amend the management of banks' recovery and resolution; the
European Parliament and the Council have adopted their respective positions.

BRIEFING
Confirmation hearings of the
Commissioners-designate


                                 Hadja Lahbib
  Preparedness and Crisis Management; Equality
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Wednesday 6 November, at 9.00.
European Parliament committees responsible: Development (DEVE), Women's Rights and Gender
Equality (FEMM), Civil Liberties, Justice and Home Affairs (LIBE), Environment, Public Health and
Food Safety (ENVI)

Hadja Lahbib a member of the Mouvement reformateur (MR) party, which sits with the Renew
Europe group in the European Parliament, has been serving as Belgian minister for foreign affairs,
European affairs and foreign trade, and federal cultural institutions since 2022.
She was elected as a member of the Parliament of the Brussels Capital Region in 2024 but is currently
on leave to fulfil her ministerial duties.
Before joining the Belgian government, she co-led the Brussels bid for the title of European Capital
of Culture 2030.
Born in Belgium in 1970 to a family of Algerian Kabyle origin, Lahbib obtained a master's degree in
journalism and communication from the Université Libre de Bruxelles. She then worked as a
journalist and presenter for Belgium's French-speaking public broadcasting company and other
broadcasters, where she gained international experience in covering conflict zones, including
Afghanistan, Iraq, Israel and the occupied Palestinian territories. She produced several
documentaries to highlight injustices, especially those against women in Afghanistan, Kenya and
Belgium.



                                             IN THIS BRIEFING
                                                 Fundamentals of the portfolio
                                                 Priorities and challenges
                                                 European Parliament
                                                 Recent developments




Hadja Lahbib, Belgium.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:
https://epthinktank.eu/commissioner_hearings_2024.


                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                 Authors: Enrico D’Ambrogio, Eric Pichon and Ionel Zamfir
                               Members' Research Service
                               PE 762.440 – October 2024                                                EN
                                                    94
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Preparedness and crisis management. Climate change has increased the frequency and severity
of natural disasters in the EU. The European Commission's Directorate-General for European Civil
Protection and Humanitarian Aid Operations (ECHO) assesses disaster risks and coordinates
preparedness actions for natural and human-caused disasters. While EU Member States carry the
primary responsibility for civil protection, all of them – plus Iceland, Norway and most candidate
countries – contribute to the Union Civil Protection Mechanism (UCPM). This mechanism has
significantly enhanced disaster-response capabilities and strengthened cooperation to assist
disaster-stricken countries within and beyond the EU (such as Ukraine). The Emergency Response
Coordination Centre (ERCC) within ECHO serves as the UCPM operational hub that monitors
emergencies in the EU and around the globe. The European Civil Protection Pool (ECPP) brings
together resources for the UCPM from Member States and other participating states.
Humanitarian crises are driven mainly by conflict (in 2023, Sudan alone accounted for almost 40 %
of global need, while the number of civilians killed in the Occupied Palestinian Territories at the end
of 2023 was equivalent to 60 % of the global civilian death toll for 2022), climate emergency and
economic factors. Most of these crises are long-standing and entail massive displacement, acute
food insecurity and grave disease outbreaks. The number of people worldwide needing humanitarian
aid rose to nearly 300 million in 2023, and more than a third of them cannot be helped because of a
funding gap. Violations of international humanitarian law have become more frequent, including
attacks against aid workers. EU and Member States' humanitarian aid is founded on the international
humanitarian principles of humanity, neutrality, impartiality and independence. ECHO has a network
in 40 non-EU countries but does not intervene directly on the ground; it funds projects run by around
200 partners. EU humanitarian assistance has an €11.6 billion budget for the 2021-2027 period. In
addition, the Solidarity and Emergency Aid Reserve allocates up to €1.2 billion per year for
responding to natural disasters and health crises. Together with its Member States, the EU is the
largest humanitarian aid donor in the world (providing over a third of global humanitarian
assistance); the European Commission alone provides 11.1 % of global humanitarian funding.
Equality is one of the EU's fundamental values. The EU Treaties allow the EU to adopt non-
discrimination laws for specific protected grounds (racial or ethnic origin, religion or belief, disability,
age, sex and sexual orientation). EU legislation also protects victims of crime and qualifies serious
manifestations of racism and xenophobia as criminal offences. To enhance equality and non-
discrimination in the Member States, the EU also uses recommendations, funding and exchanges of
good practice. Moreover, the Treaties require the EU to mainstream equality and non-discrimination
in all its policies. To monitor progress, the EU has established the European Fundamental Rights
Agency (FRA) and the European Institute for Gender Equality (EIGE). EIGE's gender equality index
and FRA's surveys reveal the extent of discrimination and inequality across the EU.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
Following the COVID-19 pandemic, 9 out of 10 respondents to a Eurobarometer survey conducted in 2023
wanted to see a stronger EU role in crisis management. The same proportion of respondents believed that
funding EU humanitarian aid was important and either approved of the current level of spending or wished to
see it increased. Seven out of 10 found agreed aid was more efficient when coordinated at the EU level.
Participating in another 2023 survey, most respondents considered discrimination to still be widespread in
their country, particularly on grounds such as Roma origin (65 %), skin colour (61 %), ethnic origin (60 %),
gender identity (being transgender (57 %)) or sexual orientation (54 %). Some 38 % believed discrimination
based on sex to be widespread (up 3 % since 2019). Around 20 % had personally experienced discrimination
or been harassed because of their age, gender, political opinions, socio-economic situation or appearance.




2
                                                       95
                                                                Preparedness and Crisis Management; Equality


Preparedness and crisis management. Hadja Lahbib will be responsible for strengthening EU civil
preparedness for and resilience to a range of increasing risks, from climate hazard to military threat.
The UCPM has shown its ability to adapt to unexpected events, but large-scale crises require better
cross-sectoral coordination and awareness, including defence and health aspects. However, ECHO
and national authorities are facing resource constraints and a significant workload. At global level,
Lahbib will have to harness the EU's humanitarian response. More specifically, this will involve
addressing the funding gap, including by leveraging private funding. The funding gap risks further
aggravating some crises, which, despite being long-standing and complex, have been forgotten by
the media and donors. To address these challenges, Lahbib will have to work towards integrating
humanitarian interventions with longer-term development, peace, disaster-risk reduction and
climate programmes. Increasing the number of global contributors and finding common ground with
other (potential) donors will also be a crucial part of her work.
Equality. According to the 2024 FRA report, discrimination is a pervasive issue disproportionately
affecting people of African descent and Roma, and incidents of racism and related forms of
intolerance are rising. EIGE's Gender Equality Index indicates that gender gaps exist in most areas
of life. Transposing directives adopted during the Commission's last term into national law should
help here. However, there is a significant gap in legal protection against discrimination in the EU, as
the proposal for a horizontal anti-discrimination directive (outside employment and on protected
grounds other than race), initiated in 2008, remains blocked in the Council.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament (EP) competence
Article 196 TFEU is the legal basis for civil protection; the EU supports Member States' actions (Article 6) and
the 'solidarity clause' applies (Article 222). Article 214 is the legal basis for humanitarian aid. Parliament and
the Council co-decide on measures aimed at achieving humanitarian and civil protection goals, although
Member States can adopt their own legal provisions in these areas. Parliament oversees the Commission's
implementing measures. The Committees on Development (DEVE) and on the Environment, Public Health and
Food Safety (ENVI) have responsibilities for humanitarian aid and civil protection, respectively. The ordinary
legislative procedure is used for anti-discrimination measures on grounds of sex in employment (Article 157),
while for grounds other than sex, Parliament must consent to a unanimous decision by the Council (Article 19).

Preparedness and crisis management. Parliament has called for the establishment of a permanent
EU civil protection force and an EU centre of excellence in civil protection. It has highlighted the
importance of ensuring adequate EU firefighting capabilities for forest fires in the EU. Since the EU-
level firefighting fleet will not be fully operational by the end of 2024, Parliament has agreed that
the EU should continue financing the leased aircraft to assist Member States in combating wildfires
until 2027. Parliament has also called for increased predictable and flexible funding for humanitarian
aid, for the inclusion of IHL violations as a criterion for sanctions, and for a clear humanitarian-
development-peace nexus policy. Additionally, it has urged the sharing of knowledge with local
stakeholders, and the establishment of an EU centre for humanitarian research.
Equality: Parliament played a key role in recent legislative initiatives on gender equality. In many
resolutions, it has called for better integration of gender equality in policy areas such as transport,
environment, digital, and budget, with a focus on women in vulnerable situations. Parliament has
urged better consideration of intersectional discrimination in both legislative and non-legislative
measures. It has called for the adoption of a new 2025-2030 LGBTIQ+ equality strategy and an
implementation plan to include consultation with LGBTIQ+ organisations. Parliament has also called
for action to combat cyber-violence against women and criminalise the demand for prostitution. It
has called for sexual and reproductive rights to be upheld throughout the EU. As hate crimes based
on religion are often under-reported and not prosecuted, Parliament has called for the
establishment of comprehensive data collection systems on hate crimes and other discriminatory
acts against belief- or religious communities. Parliament has also asked for hate speech and crimes
to be considered EU crimes, as this would allow the Commission to propose directives on the matter.



                                                                                                                3
                                                    96
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Recent developments
Preparedness and crisis management. In April 2020, responding to the pandemic, the EU activated
the Emergency Support Instrument (ESI), complemented by a dedicated reserve of response
capacities at EU level (rescEU). Building on experience from the pandemic, in May 2021 the Council
and Parliament strengthened the UCPM to enable the Commission to procure rescEU capacities
directly. RescEU has since been extended until the end of 2027. In 2021, the Commission advocated
prioritising local humanitarian responders, simplifying needs assessment and implementing flexible
funding arrangements, including digital cash transfers. It proposed strengthening coordination with
others, such as the military, and highlighted climate change, health, food security and disaster
preparedness as priorities. It emphasised the need to address the rise in violations of international
humanitarian law globally. Since 2021, a Response Capacity has helped Member States and others
coordinate their humanitarian response, and since 2022, young volunteers can join humanitarian aid
operations in non-EU countries. An annual European humanitarian forum started in 2022.
Equality. Several important laws have been adopted and comprehensive strategies to fight
discrimination proposed recently. Two new directives aim to empower women economically. The
first, the 2022 Women on Boards Directive, addresses the under-representation of women at the
highest level of economic decision-making. The second, the 2023 Equal Pay Directive, requires
companies to share information on salaries and compensate victims of pay discrimination. To do
more against gender-based violence, the EU ratified the Istanbul Convention in 2023 and adopted
a directive in May 2024 setting minimum EU-level standards for several gender-based crimes and
cybercrimes, and enhancing access to justice, protection of victims and prevention measures.
Another two new directives adopted in May 2024 propose EU standards to strengthen the role of
national equality bodies. In 2020, to protect LGBTIQ+ people's rights, the Commission tabled the
2020-2025 LGBTIQ equality strategy – the first comprehensive EU strategy in this area. The Court
of Justice ruled on several cases concerning LGBTIQ+ rights during previous terms. Other cases are
pending, including an infringement case against Hungary concerning a propaganda law.
The EU has enhanced the fundamental rights of the most vulnerable groups, in particular persons
with disabilities, and children. A draft directive establishing an EU-wide disability card and improving
the existing European parking card seeks to enhance recognition of disability status and facilitate
the free movement of persons with disabilities. In September 2020, the Commission adopted the
EU anti-racism action plan 2020-2025, and in 2021 appointed its first Anti-Racism Coordinator. An
evaluation of the Racial Equality Directive found that there is significant under-reporting and that
data collection on discrimination cases still needs improvement.

FURTHER READING
European Parliament, Hadja Lahbib hearing documents and CV, 2024.

                                    Author: Martina Prpic
                                 Members' Research Service
                                 PE 762.429 – October 2024                                             EN
                                                    98
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
The Internal Affairs and Migration portfolio encompasses a number of areas, including: internal
security; migration and asylum; law enforcement cooperation; Schengen; borders and visa policy;
dialogue under Article 17 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU) with
churches, religious associations or communities, and philosophical and non-confessional
organisations; and the fight against antisemitism and anti-Muslim hatred.
Work in recent years in the area of EU migration policy brought about the adoption in spring 2024
of the new pact on migration and asylum, seeking chiefly to alleviate the pressure placed on the EU
by the consequences of Russia's war on Ukraine. Legal migration has been another area of concern.
Recurrent terrorist attacks in the EU, as well as the evolving nature of organised crime, have
highlighted the need for better cooperation on internal security issues. Priority areas for EU action
include fighting organised crime, human trafficking, cybercrime, terrorism and radicalisation.
The protection of the EU's external borders and the maintenance of the Schengen area of free
movement are significant aspects of this portfolio. In March 2024, the Schengen area expanded to
include air travel to and from Bulgaria and Romania. However, the previous legislative term also
brought several challenges in maintaining open borders, including the impact of the COVID-19
pandemic and the influx of migrants and asylum-seekers into the EU.
Designated coordinators, who help the Commissioners responsible, work on countering
antisemitism and anti-Muslim hatred, and maintaining dialogue with churches, religious associations
or communities and philosophical and non-confessional organisations. A surge in hate speech and
hate crime in Europe, targeting the Jewish and Muslim communities in the wake of the terrorist
attacks by Hamas in Israel, followed by Israel's military response in Gaza, has prompted a new call
for action.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In the July 2024 Eurobarometer survey on EU challenges and priorities irregular migration ranked as the most
pressing issue that the EU should address for 33 % of respondents. It was chosen by 41 % of respondents as
the EU's second biggest challenge. Terrorism and security rank fifth among EU challenges (29 %). In the
European Parliament's spring 2024 Eurobarometer survey respondents considered migration and asylum to
be the seventh most important topic to be discussed as a matter of priority during the European Parliament
election campaign, and the fight against terrorism and organised crime the tenth. The standard Eurobarometer
101 survey, also published in the spring of 2024, revealed that two in three respondents (67 %) support a
common migration policy, and less than half (46 %) view immigration from outside the EU positively.

According to Commission President-elect Ursula von der Leyen's political guidelines for the next
European Commission, security and migration will receive significant attention in the next 5 years.
Her mission letter to Magnus Brunner, entrusted with the Internal Affairs and Migration portfolio,
offers greater detail on his specific tasks. For instance, he will be responsible for drafting a new
European internal security strategy to ensure that security is built into EU laws and policies, and
addresses all threats, whether online or offline. The current rules on organised crime will be revised
Europol's capacity is to be bolstered and its oversight and mandate strengthened. Other tasks
include developing a new European plan against drug trafficking, an EU action plan on firearms and
further efforts to combat cybercrime. Work will continue on protecting children against sexual
abuse online and offline. A new counter-terrorism agenda will address new and emerging threats,
especially online, and take a stronger approach to terrorist financing and anti-radicalisation. A new
European critical communication system is also planned, to be used by public authorities in charge
of security and safety. Plans also include ensuring appropriate tools to combat antisemitism and
anti-Muslim hatred, as well as continuing the Article 17 TFEU dialogue with churches, religious
associations or communities and philosophical and non-confessional organisations.



2
                                                      99
                                                                                 Internal Affairs and Migration


The political guidelines also stress the need to make EU borders more secure and efficient, with the
goal of establishing a fully functional EU digital border management system. Brunner will promote
an integrated approach to border management, which includes strengthening the European Border
and Coast Guard Agency (Frontex) and tripling the number of its border guards. Increased efforts
will be invested in cooperation with non-EU countries on border security through the development
of an EU visa policy strategy. Hybrid attacks and the instrumentalisation of migrants are highlighted
as an area of concern, as is the task of ensuring a fully functioning Schengen area of free movement.
The Commission has announced plans to step up implementation of the pact on migration and
asylum, guided by a migration and asylum strategy. A fresh common approach to returns will include
a new legal framework to expedite and simplify the process. The fight against smugglers and human
traffickers will be intensified, as will initiatives to prevent the exploitation of workers with irregular
status. Strategic partnerships on migration and security with non-EU countries will continue to be
developed, including through a new pact for the Mediterranean. The Commission also intends to
address irregular migration and support legal migration based on the skills needed within the EU.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
The EU and its Member States share competence on migration as part of justice and home affairs. Article 78
of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU) sets out the role of the European Parliament
and the Council as co-legislators when adopting measures for a common European asylum system. In the
event of a sudden influx of third-country nationals into one or more Member States, the Council can adopt
temporary measures based on a Commission proposal and after consulting the European Parliament. Migration
policy is governed by Articles 67(2), 79 and 80 TFEU; management of the external border by Articles 67(2)
and 77 TFEU; and asylum policy by Articles 67(2) and 78 TFEU. The right to asylum is enshrined in Article 18
of the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights. Moreover, EU asylum legislation must align with the international
refugee law framework established by the 1951 Geneva Convention and its Protocol, which have been
incorporated into EU law under Article 78(1) TFEU.
Most migration and home affairs issues fall under the ordinary legislative procedure. However, Article 4(2)
TEU provides that ensuring territorial integrity, maintaining law and order and safeguarding national security
are essential state functions to be respected by the EU, with only the latter of these being the sole
responsibility of each Member State. Article 83(1) TFEU provides an exception to this principle, allowing for
the adoption of directives related to minimum rules on serious crimes with a cross-border dimension under
the ordinary legislative procedure. The list of crimes eligible for this procedure may be expanded, as seen with
the recent inclusion of the violation of EU sanctions. Denmark and Ireland have specific opt-outs as provided
for in Protocols 21 (for Ireland) and 22 (for Denmark) annexed to the EU Treaties. Article 17 TFEU serves as
the legal basis for an open, transparent and regular dialogue between the EU institutions and churches,
religious associations, and philosophical and non-confessional organisations.

Parliament has been very active, both in finalising Commission proposals and setting new ambitious
targets for future work. For example, in January 2024 the Parliament adopted a resolution to extend
the list of EU crimes to include hate speech and hate crime. The resolution urged the Council to
adopt a decision to include these crimes on the list covered by Article 83(1) TFEU. Parliament has
also repeatedly expressed concern for children's safety, especially online, calling for urgent action.
Additionally, it has shown concern at the rise of antisemitism and anti-Muslim hatred in the EU. The
Parliament strongly supported Romania and Bulgaria's accession to the Schengen area.
Legal migration has also been the topic of several resolutions, with the Parliament advocating more
legislation in this area to fulfil the EU's goal of a comprehensive approach to migration policy. The
Parliament has advocated the establishment of a talent pool. It welcomed the revised Blue Card
Directive but noted that it does not address the need for low and medium-skilled workers. In this
connection, Parliament has stressed the need to attract more self-employed persons and
entrepreneurs. In a June 2023 resolution, Members also voiced concerns over the
instrumentalisation of migrants as part of hybrid foreign interference strategies, including
disinformation campaigns aimed at polarising societies and undermining fundamental rights.



                                                                                                              3
                                                    100
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Recent developments
In spring 2024, the Council and Parliament agreed on a new pact on migration and asylum, proposed
by the Commission in September 2020. The texts adopted cover asylum and migration management,
asylum procedures, the screening of third-country nationals at national borders, Eurodac, and the
Crisis and Force Majeure Regulation. The revised Schengen Borders Code, the revised Schengen
evaluation mechanism and a new Regulation as regards the Digitalisation of the EU Visa Procedure
were also adopted. Work is under way on a package of legislative proposals from November 2023,
to prevent and combat migrant smuggling. The package consists of a proposal for a directive on
preventing and countering the facilitation of unauthorised entry and stay in the EU, as well as a
regulation to strengthen Europol's role in combating migrant smuggling and human trafficking.
Additionally, work needs to continue on the recast of the Directive on common standards and
procedures in Member States for returning illegally staying third-country nationals, which was
proposed in 2018. The Council reached its position in 2019, but the Parliament has not yet done so.
Work on the package of measures on legal migration, tabled in April 2022, will also continue in the
new term. While the revised Single Permit Directive was adopted in the spring of 2024,
interinstitutional negotiations are ongoing for the Long-term Residents Directive. The proposal to
establish an EU talent pool from November 2023 has also not been agreed upon yet. Work on the
revision of the visa suspension mechanism, proposed in October 2023, will also continue in the next
term. The Council adopted its position on this mechanism in March 2024.
Work on internal security in the EU has been mapped out in the 2020-2025 EU security union
strategy. Much progress has been made in recent years, including strengthening the mandates of
Europol and the EU Drugs Agency. The EU police cooperation code package has also been adopted,
along with the revised Directive on Combating Human Trafficking and the revised Directive on Asset
Recovery and Confiscation. However, some major files are still pending, such as the proposal for
permanent rules to combat child sexual abuse online. Parliament adopted its position on these rules
in November 2023.
