                              Jessika Roswall
             Environment, Water Resilience and a
               Competitive Circular Economy
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Tuesday 5 November, at 18.30.
European Parliament committee responsible: Environment, Public Health and Food Safety (ENVI)

Jessika Roswall is a politician from the Moderate Party in Sweden, affiliated to the European People's
Party (EPP).
Prior to her nomination for the post of Commissioner, Roswall was Sweden's minister for European
affairs, from October 2022 to September 2024.
Between 2010 and 2022, she served as a member of the Swedish Parliament, holding the position of
second vice-president of its EU affairs committee from 2019 to 2022.
Roswall was also a member of the 'transparency councils' of the Swedish Consumer Agency (2015-
2018), the County Administrative Board of Uppsala (2016-2022), and the Authority for Work
Environment Expertise (2018-2019).
Born in 1972 in the county of Uppsala, Jessika Roswall holds a law degree from Uppsala University.
After graduating in 2002, she worked as a lawyer for the law firm Wigert & Placht, from 2002 to 2010.




                                                       IN THIS BRIEFING
                                                          Fundamentals of the portfolio
                                                          Priorities and challenges
                                                          European Parliament
                                                          Recent developments




Jessika Roswall, Sweden.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:
https://epthinktank.eu/commissioner_hearings_2024.



                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                        Authors: Camille Siefridt and Vivienne Halleux
                                 Members' Research Service
                                 PE 762.438 – October 2024                                               EN
                                                      102
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Based on the precaution, prevention, rectifying pollution at the source, and 'polluter pays' principles,
EU environmental policy is guided by multiannual environment action programmes (EAPs) that set
out priority objectives to be attained. The current programme (eighth EAP) runs until 2030. EU
environmental protection legislation includes instruments to tackle air, water and soil pollution, to
prevent and control industrial emissions, to regulate chemicals, to ensure proper waste management
and move towards a more circular economy, as well as to preserve nature and biodiversity. The EU
is a party to many international environmental agreements. Funding for environment-related
projects is provided through the LIFE programme, which has a budget of €5.4 billion for 2021-2027.
The European Union and the wider world are in the midst of a triple environmental crisis involving
climate change, biodiversity loss and pollution. The European Green Deal was designed as a holistic
strategy to address these interconnected challenges.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In a May 2024 Eurobarometer survey, over 75 % of the EU citizens polled thought environmental issues had a
direct impact on their daily lives, while 84 % agreed that EU environmental legislation was necessary to protect
the environment in their country. Most respondents (84 %) were worried about the impact of harmful chemicals
found in everyday products on both human health and the environment. Over half (52 %) considered that the
current level of protection from hazardous chemicals was too low. Around 6 in 10 respondents identified plastic
waste (61 %) and chemical waste (60 %) as the most problematic waste types in their country. Respondents
strongly agreed (92 %) that companies should pay the costs of their pollution. Lastly, 78 % of respondents wanted
the EU to introduce additional measures to address water-related problems in the EU.

EU environment policy seeks to ensure that by 2050 at the latest, people can live well within the
limits of the planet. Research shows that six out of nine planetary boundaries have already been
crossed, particularly in the areas of chemicals, biosphere integrity and freshwater. A recent global
foresight report identified a number of global shifts, including humanity's relationship with the
environment, increased competition for critical resources and rapid technological development such
as digitalisation and artificial intelligence, which are accelerating the environmental triple crisis. The
first monitoring report of the European Environment Agency (EEA) on progress towards the eighth
EAP objectives concludes that meeting EU environment policy targets by 2030 will be challenging.
Twenty out of the 28 headline indicators in the monitoring framework are not on track to reach their
2030 objectives. In its July 2024 report on Europe's sustainability transitions outlook, the EEA
stresses the importance of anchoring environmental goals in the EU's strategic priorities for the next
political cycle, focusing in particular on competitiveness, security and fairness, in order for the EU to
stay on track towards its long-term vision of living well within the planetary boundaries.
In her mission letter to Jessika Roswall, Ursula von der Leyen – Commission President-elect – asks
her to develop new initiatives, including a circular economy act, with measures to create market
demand for secondary materials and a single market for waste, also focusing on critical raw
materials. Roswall is also tasked with developing a chemicals industry package, to simplify REACH
and provide clarity on per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS); as well as a European water
resilience strategy. Roswall will be in charge of updating the bioeconomy strategy and coordinating
the new European Bauhaus, while also contributing to the new climate adaptation plan and vision
for agriculture and food. A key priority for Roswall will be to ensure proper implementation and
enforcement of environment, nature and zero-pollution legislation. A 2019 study for the Commission
estimated the costs of not implementing EU environmental law at around €55 billion per year.
The mission letter specifically mandates Roswall to take the EU's zero pollution ambition forward.
Some key steps were taken on chemicals in the last term, such as the revision of the CLP Regulation,
the 'universal' PFAS restriction proposal under REACH, currently being evaluated by the European




2
                                                      103
                                          Environment, Water Resilience and a Competitive Circular Economy


Chemicals Agency, and the presentation of the 'one substance, one assessment' legislative package.
However, the revision of the REACH Regulation itself, originally announced for the end of 2022, then
delayed to the end of 2023, is overdue. The first synthesis report on the EU indicator framework for
chemicals highlights the need to accelerate the shift to safe and sustainable chemicals, and to
further reduce the impact of harmful substances on human health and the environment. On pollution
more generally, there is a strong signal that the application of the 'polluter pays' principle needs to
be improved (widespread PFAS pollution, European Court of Auditors' report). Yet the Commission
still has to deliver its evaluation of the 2004 Environmental Liability Directive, which should have
been released by 2023. Parliament called for a swift revision and transformation of the directive into
a fully harmonised regulation applying to all companies operating in the EU.
Under two successive action plans (2015 and 2020), the EU has put measures in place to enable a
transition towards a more circular economy and thereby alleviate pressures on climate and natural
resources. Though progress has been made, for instance on recycling and on developing the sharing
economy, there is still a long way to go to achieve the ambition of doubling the EU's circularity rate
by 2030. Elements to consider for moving forward could include setting possible binding targets on
resource use or material footprint, improving the functioning of the single market for secondary raw
materials, increasing the uptake of EU funding for circular product design, extending the circular
approach to new sectors, and putting a stronger focus on demand-side measures. More effort is also
needed to eliminate substances of concern from waste and secondary materials.
With water resources increasingly under stress across Europe, support for action on water resilience
is also growing. Advocates of an 'EU Blue Deal', including the European Economic and Social
Committee and the European Committee of the Regions, want water to be a strategic priority for
the next Commission. The strategic agenda 2024-2029 adopted by the European Council includes a
commitment to strengthen water resilience across the EU.
Stakeholders from the entire agri-food chain involved in the strategic dialogue on the future of EU
agriculture have also made recommendations for water-resilient agriculture. Moreover, they
collectively called on the EU to act on land take, notably by establishing a legally binding objective
of 'no net land take' by 2050 and integrating it into an EU Soil Monitoring Law; and to support farmers
and other land managers in habitat restoration with a well-resourced nature restoration fund.
Securing adequate funding for implementing commitments made under the Kunming-Montreal
Global Biodiversity Framework and the EU biodiversity strategy for 2030 will be a key challenge for
the coming years. The mission letter specifically mandates Roswall to design incentives for nature-
positive actions and private investment, with a particular focus on nature credits. Actions expected
from the Commissioner at international level further include strengthening the EU's global water
leadership and striving for an ambitious global treaty on plastic pollution, as the fifth session of
negotiations approaches. The discussions on the revision of the Gothenburg Protocol under the
UNECE Air Convention are another important upcoming milestone.
The EU cannot curb the global unsustainable exploitation of the planet on its own. Ways to rise to
this challenge include enhancing EU green diplomacy, while also developing reciprocity provisions
regarding environmental obligations, building on the rules adopted for the importation of food
containing neonicotinoid residues or carbon-intensive goods.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
EU environmental policy is based on Articles 191-193 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the EU (TFEU). Article 11
TFEU also requires environmental protection to be 'mainstreamed' in other EU policies. The EU and Member
States share competence on environmental policy, which is subject to the ordinary legislative procedure (co-
decision by the Parliament and Council), with some exceptions requiring unanimity in Council (Article 192 TFEU).
Parliament must give its consent for the EU to become a party to international agreements.




                                                                                                                3
                                                   104
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


In the last term, Parliament helped shape a range of laws under the European Green Deal, securing,
for instance, the adoption of the first-ever EU legal framework to tackle EU-driven deforestation,
the prohibition of exports of non-hazardous plastic waste to non-OECD countries, and the
introduction of a ban on the use of PFAS in food-contact packaging. Parliament also adopted
first-reading positions on microplastic pollution from plastic pellets loss; soil monitoring and
resilience; and chemical pollutants listed for control in EU freshwater bodies.

Recent developments
In response to the climate and environment emergency, the von der Leyen I Commission established
the European Green Deal as the first of its six priorities for 2019-2024. The European Green Deal set
a roadmap to make Europe the first climate-neutral continent by 2050, enhance the circular
economy, cut pollution and halt biodiversity loss, while mobilising finance for the green transition.
Specific strategies translate these overarching goals into action, including the new circular economy
action plan, the chemicals strategy for sustainability, the EU biodiversity strategy for 2030, the
'farm-to-fork' strategy for sustainable food and the zero pollution action plan for air, water and soil.
To tackle biodiversity loss and environmental degradation, the EU adopted a new Nature Restoration
Law and Regulation on Deforestation-free Products. On the bid for zero pollution, several core laws
were revised, including the Industrial Emissions Directive, the Ambient Air Quality Directives and
the Directive on urban wastewater treatment. The Commission withdrew its legislative proposal on
sustainable pesticide use after Parliament rejected it, noting the need for 'a different approach'.
Action for more circularity has focused both on improving product design and end-of-life
management. The EU adopted new legislation to make batteries more sustainable and durable,
developed general eco-design requirements for sustainable products and revised existing rules on
packaging and packaging waste. It also adopted common rules promoting the repair of goods.
The EU revised its Environmental Crime Directive, with an updated list of offences and penalties.

European Parliament committees responsible: Budgets (BUDG) and Budgetary Control (CONT).

From 2012 to 2014, Serafin held the position of secretary of state for European affairs at the Polish

From 1999 to 2009, Serafin worked at the Polish Office of the Committee for European Integration,

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                    Author: Marianna Pari
                                  Members' Research Service
                                  PE 762.451 – October 2024                                             EN
                                                 106
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Compared to national budgets, the EU's annual budget is relatively small, representing around 1 %
of its Member States' gross national income (GNI), and some 2 % of total public spending in the EU.
The 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework (MFF) (the EU's long-term budget) is worth
€1 211 billion in current prices. It is supplemented by €806.9 billion from Next Generation EU
(NGEU), a novel emergency instrument designed to help mitigate the impact of the pandemic. It is
financed through resources borrowed by the EU on the financial markets. To facilitate the repayment
of the NGEU debt, a roadmap has been developed for the introduction of new own resources. EU
spending is subject to democratic scrutiny, which is implemented through a number of tools,
including the discharge procedure, through which the European Parliament holds the European
Commission politically accountable for the implementation of the EU budget. The EU's financial
interests are protected by the EU Financial Regulation and the Rule of Law Conditionality Regulation.

Priorities and challenges
In her speech before Parliament in July 2024, Commission President-elect Ursula von der Leyen
pointed out that to unleash private investment in the EU, more public funding, more investment
capacity and a reinforced budget will be necessary. She furthermore emphasised that respect for
the rule of law is a must for EU funds. With these words she underlined two key objectives of the
new Commission: to find ways to secure additional public investment in the EU, while ensuring that
there is a strong focus on the rule of law in implementation of the EU budget.
The post-2027 MFF, for which the new Commission is due to present its proposal by 1 July 2025,
will be a defining project. In her mission letter to Piotr Serafin, von der Leyen demanded a 'simpler,
more focused and responsive' long-term budget, which links reforms with investments, establishes
a European competitiveness fund and introduces new own resources. The next MFF will have to
address numerous financial challenges facing the EU, including implementing the green and digital
transitions, maintaining the EU's support for Ukraine, pursuing enlargement, enhancing EU security
and defence capabilities, and – from 2028 to 2058 – repaying the resources borrowed on the
markets to finance the NGEU grant component. Parliament is expected to present its ideas in early
2025. New own resources should cover at least the NGEU repayment costs, without reducing other
programmes or funds, as agreed in the roadmap for the reform of the EU's own resources.
The new Commission and the Member States will implement the final phase of the Recovery and
Resilience Facility (RRF), which is financed by NGEU and set to end in 2026. Scrutiny of the RRF
will remain crucial, with an ex-post evaluation scheduled by the end of 2028. Addressing
accountability gaps in the RRF setup, which have consistently been pointed out by Parliament and
the Court of Auditors, as well as the impact of increased debt resulting from large-scale borrowing
to finance NGEU and the risk of re-nationalising EU spending, will be key challenges. These factors
need to be taken into account when deciding whether the RRF can serve as a blueprint for future
financing instruments and whether more use should be made of performance-based financing.
Another task of Piotr Serafin will be to ensure a modern, efficient public administration that can
effectively deliver on the EU's priorities. One of the objectives outlined in the 2022 human resources
strategy for the Commission was to achieve full gender equality at all Commission management
levels by 2024. According to the Commission's activity report for 2022, by the end of 2022, 46 % of
management posts were held by women, with 43 % at senior management level, marking a
seven percentage-point increase from the beginning of the mandate.




2
                                                     107
                                                                Budget, Anti-Fraud and Public Administration


European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Articles 310 to 325 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU) detail the EU's financial
architecture. Parliament's powers vary depending on the procedure: while Parliament is on an equal footing
with the Council for the annual budgetary procedure (Article 314), Parliament can only give or refuse its
consent to the MFF (Article 312). Regarding the EU's own resources (Article 311), Parliament is only consulted.
The Financial Regulation and the Rule of Law Conditionality Regulation are based on Article 294, the ordinary
legislative procedure. The NGEU instrument was adopted on the basis of Article 122, which allows the Council,
on a Commission proposal, to decide on extraordinary measures in situations of crisis. Article 41 of the Treaty
on European Union (TEU) deals with financing operations under the EU's common foreign and security policy.

In the context of the MFF's revision in 2024, Parliament criticised the significant delay in the start of
dialogue with the Commission and the Council. Parliament insisted that in future, cooperation should
start at the beginning of any revision, in line with the Treaties and the interinstitutional agreement.
For Parliament, the introduction of genuine new own resources is essential for sustainable EU
financing in the future.
The political guidelines for the new Commission, emphasise the importance of respecting the rule
of law, in particular as a condition for disbursing EU funds. This is fully in line with Parliament's
stance: Parliament has repeatedly asked the Commission to move quickly to apply the Budgetary
Conditionality Regulation when breaches of the rule of law risk impacting the EU's financial interests.
Ensuring that the rule of law is upheld will continue to be a priority for Parliament in the new
legislative period, with a particular emphasis on Hungary.
Accountability and scrutiny are essential components of a functioning parliamentary democracy
and the European Parliament plays a key role in the EU's budgetary scrutiny. Parliament's new Rules
of Procedure grant additional scrutiny powers to its Committee on Budgets (BUDG) in the legislative
cycle. Rule 58 requires a budgetary assessment for all legal proposals with budgetary implications.
The discharge procedure is a significant instrument for control and scrutiny in Parliament's hands. It
involves public debates, notably hearings with Commissioners and the exchange of views with
Commission directors-general. Parliament's decisions on discharge are accompanied by a resolution
highlighting areas of concern and providing recommendations that the Commission is obliged to
address. A key element of the procedure is the annual report by the European Court of Auditors.
Rule 101 of Parliament's Rules of Procedure specifies that the discharge procedure should cover
non-traditionally financed instruments, off-budget instruments and performance-based hybrid
instruments, and that the Committee on Budgetary Control (CONT) must be involved in the
legislative work on these instruments. In line with these rules, Parliament will continue to scrutinise
implementation of the grant component of the RRF funds closely, through the discharge procedure.

Recent developments
The EU's budgetary resources for the 2021-2027 MFF, as set in December 2020, were not sufficient
to face the multiple crises at hand, most notably Russia's war against Ukraine and its consequences.
Parliament demanded an upscaling of the MFF, to allow the EU to deal effectively with the
challenges. In February 2024, Parliament and the Council agreed on an unprecedented revision of
the MFF ceilings, and secured €50 billion in financial support for Ukraine and €14.6 billion for
priorities such as migration, key technologies, and enhanced flexibility of the EU budget. At
Parliament's request, the EU recovery instrument was introduced to cover overruns in the borrowing
costs for NGEU. The EU's 2025 annual budget will be the first to be set within the new MFF ceilings.
In the relevant discussions, Parliament emphasised the importance of complying with the rule of law
and the need to ensure that financing of the costs related to NGEU do not affect programmed
expenditure.




                                                                                                             3
                                                   108
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Regarding Ukraine, on 20 September 2024 the European Commission proposed a Ukraine loan
cooperation mechanism of up to €45 billion and an exceptional macro-financial assistance (MFA)
loan of up to €35 billion. Ukraine would service and repay these loans through future flows of
extraordinary revenues stemming from frozen Russian sovereign assets held in G7 jurisdictions. On
top of that, the disbursement of the €50 billion Ukraine Facility, as defined in Regulation (EU)
2024/792, continues, contingent on Ukraine implementing the quantitative and qualitative reform
steps outlined in the Ukraine plan and adhering to EU values. So far, the EU has paid out €12 billion
to Ukraine through the facility.
The Next Generation EU recovery instrument has increased EU spending significantly. At its core,
the RRF has made €648 billion available to Member States in the form of grants or loans to finance
key investment and reform measures, not least in the green and digital transitions. The RRF has a
new delivery model that puts performance at the heart of budget spending by basing payments on
results achieved rather than costs incurred. Additionally, it is financed through EU borrowing on an
unprecedented scale. The Commission's mid-term evaluation in 2024 concluded that, while it was
too early to assess its full impact, the RRF had proven key in boosting structural reforms in the
Member States. At the same time, owing to the deadline set, implementation of NGEU has come at
the cost of delayed implementation of the cohesion funds.
The need to secure resources to finance new budgetary expenditure, notably under NGEU, led to
the adoption of the 2020 Own Resources Decision, allowing the Commission to borrow resources
from the capital markets. Subsequently, the Commission proposed new additional own resources
through the EU emissions trading scheme, the new carbon border adjustment mechanism and a
temporary statistical own resource based on company profits. While these Commission proposals
were endorsed by Parliament, the Council has so far refused to discuss them at political level.
The Budgetary Conditionality Regulation is a powerful tool to protect the EU's financial interests and
the rule of law within the EU. Its rules include the suspension of payments or commitments of EU
programmes in favour of Member States and the reduction of pre-financing, if breaches affect the
sound financial management of the EU's budget or the protection of its financial interests in a
sufficiently direct way. The regulation has been applied in relation to funding for Hungary.

Committee on Budgetary Control, 2019-2024 activity report, European Parliament, 2024.

Confirmation hearings of the

                              Dan Jørgensen
                             Energy and Housing
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Tuesday 5 November, at 14.30.
European Parliament committees responsible: Industry, Research and Energy (ITRE); Employment
and Social Affairs (EMPL)

Dan Jørgensen has most recently served as Danish Minister for Development Cooperation and
Minister for Global Climate Policy. His role has been to oversee Denmark's international
development initiatives and global climate policies, including co-facilitating COP28 climate
negotiations and co-chairing a number of working groups and alliances.
Jørgensen is a member of the Social Democratic Party in Denmark, which is affiliated to the S&D
group in the European Parliament. He has been a member of the Danish parliament since 2015 and
previously served as Minister for Food, Agriculture and Fisheries, as well as Minister for Climate,
Energy and Utilities, where he co-facilitated COP26 and COP27 negotiations and served as chair of
the International Energy Agency's Global Commission on People-Centred Clean Energy Transitions.
Jørgensen was a Member of the European Parliament (2004-2013), where he served as vice-chair of
the Committee on Environment, president of the Animal Welfare Intergroup and head of the Danish
delegation of Social Democrats (2009-2013).
Born in 1975, Jørgensen holds a master's degree in political science from Aarhus University. His
professional career also includes academic positions as adjunct professor and external lecturer.

                                                         Recent developments




Dan Jørgensen, Denmark.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:
https://epthinktank.eu/commissioner_hearings_2024.

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
               Authors: Monika Dulian, Agnieszka Widuto and Marketa Pape
                               Members' Research Service
                               PE 762.431 – October 2024                                               EN
                                                    110
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Current EU energy policy is based on the energy union strategy dating back to 2015, which aimed to
ensure an affordable, secure and sustainable energy supply for EU citizens and businesses. In recent
years, legislative activity has been guided by the European Green Deal, a set of policy initiatives
contributing to the EU's goal of becoming climate neutral by 2050. The European Climate Law,
adopted in 2021, enshrined into law the objective of a minimum 55 % reduction in net greenhouse
gas (GHG) emissions by 2030 (compared with 1990 levels) and the 2050 climate-neutrality goal.
To meet these goals and ensure a profound energy transition (from fossil fuels to low-carbon and
renewable energy sources, improving energy efficiency across economic activity), the EU decided
to overhaul its climate and energy legislation with the 'fit for 55' package proposed in July 2021 and
adopted in autumn 2023. The objectives set in the original 'fit for 55' package were modified by the
REPowerEU plan, aimed at phasing out dependency on Russian fossil fuels in response to the full-
scale Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022. The plan intended to increase the security of energy supply
by diversifying it, saving energy and boosting the clean energy transition. The price spikes and
disruptions of supply that followed the invasion required a bold crisis response by the EU.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In a 2023 Eurobarometer survey on EU priorities and challenges, 86 % of EU citizens polled supported gas
storage as a way to avoid the risk of shortages. Furthermore, 85 % of respondents believed that the EU should
boost the manufacturing of clean technologies within Member States and 79 % thought that EU measures
should focus on reducing energy consumption. The poll also indicated strong support for the sustainable use
of natural resources (86 %), for the joint gas purchase by Member States to ensure security of supply (82 %)
and for building partnerships with global energy players (81 %). In a slightly more recent Eurobarometer
survey, 83 % of respondents agreed that the EU should invest extensively in renewable energies and 81 %
believed the EU should reduce its dependency on Russian sources of energy.

In her political guidelines for the new Commission, Commission President-elect Ursula von der
Leyen highlighted the need to restore competitiveness. She proposed a clean industrial deal to
decarbonise the economy and cut energy prices by 'simplifying, investing and ensuring access to
cheap, sustainable and secure energy supplies and raw materials'. A new European competitiveness
fund will be a tool for investing in strategic technologies (e.g. clean tech), to be made in the EU. In
her mission letter to Jørgensen, von der Leyen set out various tasks. The legislative initiatives
included implementation of the 2030 framework, review of еnergy union governance, security of
supply and the 'citizens energy' package. Other initiatives included action plans on affordable energy
prices and electrification, a roadmap towards ending Russian energy imports, a clean energy
investment strategy for Europe, and a strategic roadmap for digitalisation and artificial intelligence
in the energy sector. Jørgensen is also expected to engage in improving EU grids, small modular
reactors, nuclear safety, aggregate demand mechanisms, carbon capture, utilisation and storage,
energy taxation, fossil fuel subsidies and energy diplomacy, as well as the EU vision for COP30.
High energy prices are a challenge for the EU, especially when compared with other leading
economies such as the US and China. The ongoing energy transition, based on energy efficiency,
home-grown renewables and decreased dependence on external energy suppliers, could help tackle
the high energy costs, provided the right instruments are chosen. According to EPRS estimates, EU
budgetary, regulatory and coordination actions to transform energy systems could bring in over
€464 billion per year by 2030 and €1 029 billion by 2050. Recently, industrial competitiveness has
gained prominence, with several initiatives aimed at better connecting it to climate and energy
policy (including the Net-Zero Industry Act and Critical Raw Materials Act). The Strategic Agenda
adopted by the June 2024 European Council placed a stronger focus on competitiveness compared
to the agenda adopted 5 years ago. Similar concerns were voiced during the election campaign for
the European Parliament, with citizens viewing energy policy though a prism of competitiveness.



2
                                                        111
                                                                                              Energy and Housing


European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Article 194 TFEU is the legal basis for EU energy policy. It outlines four main areas for EU action: the functioning
of the single energy market; security of energy supply; energy efficiency and renewables; and the
interconnection of energy networks. It also specifies that Member States are entitled to determine their energy
mix. Legislation under Article 194 TFEU usually follows the ordinary legislative procedure, which gives equal
weight to the Council and the Parliament. However, an exception is made for measures 'primarily of a fiscal
nature' such as energy taxation. In these cases, the Council decides alone, under a special legislative procedure
requiring unanimous agreement, with Parliament having only a consultative role.
Article 122 TFEU grants the Council the right to decide unilaterally (following a proposal by the Commission)
in cases of an economic emergency in the energy sector. This provision was used during the 2022 energy crisis,
with Council adopting several emergency regulations (see below). In recent changes to its Rules of Procedure,
the Parliament established a new procedure to follow when Article 122 TFEU is used as a legal basis (Rule 138).
This procedure provides a framework for the Commission to explain to Parliament the reasons for choosing
Article 122 and a procedure for Parliament's Committee on Legal Affairs to verify those reasons.

During its previous term, the Parliament repeatedly underlined the importance of reducing energy
consumption, keeping energy affordable, and ensuring that the burden of the green transition does
not weigh disproportionately on vulnerable citizens and regions. Parliament stressed the urgent
need to accelerate the deployment of renewables, held back by administrative obstacles and an
under-developed grid and storage infrastructure. The EU should also capitalise on the under-used
potential of the internal energy market and energy system integration. Parliament consistently
advocated a ban on the import of Russian gas and called for a comprehensive strategy for the
deployment of small modular reactors in the EU. Additionally, Parliament proposed to substantially
increase the budget of the European Hydrogen Bank, with a focus on supporting hard-to-abate
industries and heavy transport. Parliament also recommended that the Commission not only present
a geothermal strategy but also facilitate the establishment of a 'geothermal alliance'.

Recent developments
All files under the 'fit for 55' package of legislative initiatives (except the one on the energy taxation
directive) were successfully negotiated between the Parliament and the Council and entered into
force in 2023 and 2024. The gist of these initiatives is described below.
Energy efficiency. The revised Energy Efficiency Directive (EED) makes it binding for EU countries
to collectively ensure an additional 11.7 % reduction in energy consumption by 2030.
Renewables. The Renewable Energy Directive (RED) was comprehensively revised to include a
binding EU-wide target of at least 42.5 % for the share of renewables in final energy consumption
by 2030, as well as sub-targets for transport, buildings, industry, and heating and cooling.
Electricity markets. The 2024 EU electricity market reform includes two new regulations: on
electricity market design and on protection against wholesale energy market manipulation. Focused
on consumer protection, it offers more fixed-priced contracts and stronger supplier obligations. It
incentivises energy suppliers to invest in renewable and low-carbon energy by supporting the use
of long-term instruments, such as power purchase agreements and contracts for difference.
Hydrogen and gas markets. This package consists of the revised Gas Regulation and Gas Directive.
It aims to facilitate the integration of renewable and low-carbon gases into the existing gas network,
and create a market for hydrogen, including by building a cross-border hydrogen infrastructure.
These texts also pave the way for a permanent gas demand aggregation platform. To ensure the
phase-out of fossil fuels, long-term contracts for fossil gas must end no later than December 2049.
Energy crisis response. Along with REPowerEU, a series of emergency Council regulations were
adopted. These include regulations on gas consumption reduction (by 15 %); on an emergency



                                                                                                                  3
                                                    112
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


intervention to address high energy prices, establishing a common gas purchase platform (the
Solidarity Regulation); on accelerating the deployment of renewable energy and on establishing a
market correction mechanism. Only the Storage Regulation, requiring Member States to fill their gas
storage to 90 % before winter, was adopted under the ordinary legislative procedure with
Parliament's involvement.

Housing
As housing affordability has become a growing issue across the EU, housing has been included in
Jorgensen's portfolio. The EU has no direct competence in housing, but it supports Member States
through recommendations, data collection, exchange of best practice, and funding. The EU has an
indirect influence on housing provision as well through its fiscal, competition and State aid rules. The
right to housing is built into the European Pillar of Social Rights and was further developed in the EU
Urban Agenda. The recently adopted Climate Law has given momentum to the renovation wave
strategy (including an affordable housing initiative), aimed at improving the energy efficiency of
buildings, reducing energy poverty and improving housing quality. The revised Energy Performance
of Buildings Directive (EPBD) sets a goal to fully decarbonise buildings by 2050. It mandates that all
new residential buildings should be zero-emission by 2030 and new public buildings by 2028. The
EPBD also encourages the phasing-out of boilers powered by fossil fuels and their replacement with
solar thermal boilers or heat pumps. It also promotes the decarbonisation of electricity systems.
The combating homelessness platform supports various activities such as evidence-gathering,
mutual learning and schemes facilitating access to finance. Jørgensen is tasked with preparing a
European affordable housing plan. Another of his tasks is to help develop an EU strategy for housing
construction to support housing supply, reduce building costs, ensure enough skilled workers, and
improve productivity and environmental performance of construction. Addressing systemic issues
with short-term accommodation rentals is yet another area he must tackle. With the current EU
budget set to be renegotiated by 2027 and a new economic governance framework in place,
Jørgensen is also tasked with helping Member States double their investment in affordable housing
under cohesion policy, and with creating, in partnership with the European Investment Bank, an
investment platform for affordable and sustainable housing.

Confirmation hearings of the

                         Ekaterina Zaharieva
              Start-ups, Research and Innovation
Confirmation hearing due to be held on Tuesday 5 November, at 9.00.
European Parliament committee responsible: Industry, Research and Energy (ITRE)

Ekaterina Zaharieva is currently a member of the Bulgarian National Assembly, representing the
Citizens for European Development of Bulgaria party (EPP).
Having served in several Bulgarian governments, Zaharieva held the dual position of deputy prime
minister for judicial reform and minister of foreign affairs from 2017 to 2021. Earlier, she served as
minister of justice from 2015 to 2017, as deputy prime minister for economic policy and minister of
regional developments and public works in 2013 and 2014, and as deputy minister of regional
development and public works from 2009 to 2011. Between 2011 and 2015, she acted as head of
cabinet and secretary general to the President of the Republic of Bulgaria.
After earning a master's degree in law from Paisii Hilendarski University in Plovdiv, Zaharieva had
followed a career as a lawyer, and then as a civil servant from 2017 to 2021.
Zaharieva was born in Pazardzhik, Bulgaria, in 1975.

                                                    Fundamentals of the portfolio
                                                    Priorities and challenges
                                                    European Parliament
                                                    Recent developments




Ekaterina Zaharieva, Bulgaria.

This is one of a set of briefings designed to give an overview of issues of interest relating to the
portfolios of the Commissioners-designate. All these briefings can be found at:
https://epthinktank.eu/commissioner_hearings_2024.



                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                   Author: Clement Evroux
                                  Members' Research Service
                                  PE 762.442 – October 2024                                              EN
                                                  114
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Research and innovation are major drivers of prosperity through several complementary channels.
New knowledge and solutions can create new market opportunities, while also increasing the
productivity of the economy as a whole. According to the latest data from Eurostat, research and
development (R&D) intensity in the EU (the EU's gross domestic R&D expenditure as a percentage
of gross domestic product – GDP) amounted to 2.24 % (with large differences between Member
States) in 2022. According to 2021 figures, the EU's main international partners invest a greater share
of their GDP: the United States invests 3.4 % and South Korea 4.9 %. This figure is far below the 3 %
target first set by the European Council in 2002 (to be reached by 2010). Beyond the size of the gap,
its structure also matters. In 2022, while the EU and US shared a similar intensity of public spending
in research and innovation (R&I), EU business expenditure on R&D (1.48 % of GDP) amounted to only
slightly more than half the corresponding figure for the US (2.68 % of GDP). This situation has several
causes, including the limited capacity of EU business ecosystems to absorb new solutions, such as
ICT innovations.
R&I is also a key policy domain for addressing global challenges such as health, climate change and
biodiversity loss. The magnitude of these challenges calls for cooperation across scientific
disciplines, economic sectors, and borders, to ensure evidence-based policymaking. This is
particularly important for optimising EU crisis preparedness and response.
In the current context of geopolitical tensions, R&I is also essential for strengthening EU strategic
autonomy, not least through its capacity to create new knowledge and skills, and by attracting global
talents to the EU. It is important to establish the necessary framework conditions for scientific and
innovation creation, while also safeguarding against specific risks such as foreign interference.
The mission letter to Zaharieva contains a set of EU legislative initiatives. These include a European
research area act, a European innovation act and an advanced materials act. These initiatives
represent a policy precedent in this sector, as EU legislative initiatives have historically focused on
EU framework programmes for R&I. The mission letter also invites Zaharieva to expand the European
Research Council and the European Innovation Council in order to create an EU artificial intelligence
research council, whilst ensuring the financial sustainability of EU R&I infrastructure, reinforcing
international cooperation and supporting young researchers.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
According to the standard Eurobarometer 101 survey conducted in April and May 2024, around 12 % of EU
respondents believe that inventions, science and technology contribute the most to a feeling of community
among EU citizens, ahead of economy, healthcare and sports.

As highlighted in the single market report by Enrico Letta, and then in Mario Draghi's report on EU
competitiveness, the two main challenges of EU R&I policy are: i) reversing the declining productivity
of the EU economy and closing the competitiveness gap with other global scientific and economic
powers; and ii) reducing the fragmentation of the European research area to allow for the free
circulation of knowledge and researchers, referred as the 'fifth freedom' in the Letta report.
Against this backdrop, and taking into account the outcome of the interim evaluation of Horizon
Europe, the ninth EU framework programme for research and innovation, the Commission is
expected to publish its proposal for the next EU framework programme in 2025. While a majority of
stakeholders call for continuity in the architecture and rules, the vast majority also favour a budget
increase, which may require a reduction in the number of the programme's priorities.
A last set of challenges relates to promoting the framework conditions needed to facilitate the
creation of knowledge and its dissemination. This includes open data, intellectual property and
regulatory sandboxes.



2
                                                    115
                                                                         Start-ups, Research and Innovation


European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Article 3(3) of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU) specifies that the Union 'shall
promote scientific and technological advance'. Article 179 TFEU sets the objective to achieve a European
research area where 'researchers, scientific knowledge and technology circulate freely', together with the
objective of strengthening the EU's scientific and technological bases. In addition to the EU framework
programme for research and innovation (currently Horizon Europe), the EU can, applying the ordinary
legislative procedure, adopt measures in addition to the framework programmes, whenever such measures
are necessary for the implementation of the European research area.

Parliament fulfils its role as co-legislator by examining relevant legislative proposals (such as on
Horizon Europe and on the Strategic Technologies for Europe Platform (STEP) Regulation). It also
scrutinises Horizon Europe's implementation through a dedicated working group set up by the
Committee on Industry, Research and Energy (ITRE). It then conveys horizontal policy messages and
instructions to the Commission and the Council through its resolutions.
In a resolution of 17 January 2024 on the promotion of the freedom of scientific research in the EU,
Parliament requested the Commission to submit a legislative proposal to safeguard the freedom of
scientific research among academic research organisations. In a resolution of 9 May 2023 on critical
technologies for security and defence, Parliament welcomed the Commission proposal to bridge the
current divide between civil, defence and security research, development and innovation. In a
resolution of 8 April 2022 on a global approach to research and innovation (R&I), Parliament
expressed support for the openness of EU R&I. Noting the new framework for association with EU
framework programmes, it called on the Council and the Commission to enhance its role in the
implementation of such association agreements.

Recent developments
On the institutional side, between 2019 and 2024, in line with the shared competence on EU R&I
policy, the EU carried out two main complementary activities: a) adopting and implementing Horizon
Europe; and b) adopting and implementing the new policy agenda on the European research area.
Horizon Europe invests in R&I activities across all scientific and industrial sectors, while the initiatives
on the European research area aim to coordinate Member States' scientific policy initiatives,
including in the area of scientific employment and knowledge valorisation.

Horizon Europe
The 2021 regulation, which established Horizon Europe, laid down the following general objective
for the programme: 'to deliver scientific, technological, economic and societal impact ... to
strengthen the scientific and technological bases of the Union ... and to contribute to the realisation
of Union objectives and policies'. The programme is subdivided into three main parts or 'pillars':
(pillar I, excellent science; pillar II, global challenges and European industrial leadership; pillar III,
innovative Europe), and a horizontal part (European research area, widening participation).
The available budget for 2021-2027 is €95.5 billion (2018 prices), which corresponds roughly to a
range of around 9-11 % of the corresponding expenditure of Member States (including the higher
education sector) on an annual basis. Since 2021, Horizon Europe activities have supported more
than 11 500 projects, gathering more than 78 000 participants, including more than 15 000 SMEs,
through an EU contribution of up to €33.59 billion (data available on 26 June 2024).
Horizon also contributes to the overarching political priorities of the EU. For health and resilience,
to ensure EU preparedness and response, Horizon Europe funds a European partnership on
pandemic preparedness to federate the relevant EU scientific and medical institutions, based on a
common research and innovation agenda. For the ecological transition, by 2027 Horizon Europe is
expected to have invested €1 billion in climate science to provide the knowledge basis needed to



                                                                                                          3
                                                    116
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


implement evidence-based policies. 35 % of the Europe Horizon budget will be directed towards
climate-friendly projects. For the digital transition, Horizon Europe supports several kinds of
emerging technologies of relevance to EU strategic autonomy, such as supercomputing and
quantum. To advance knowledge and the uptake of results and solutions across these priorities,
international cooperation is a strong asset. Since the launch of Horizon Europe, 18 third countries,
including Israel, the United Kingdom and New Zealand have been associated with Horizon Europe,
while Canada's association is expected to be applicable by the end of 2024.
In the context of geopolitical tensions, the EU's global approach to research and innovation has also
developed safeguards against foreign interference in EU scientific endeavours, including a proposal
for a Council recommendation on research security.

European research area (ERA)
The Commission presented its 2019-2024 priorities in the 2020 communication on a new ERA for
R&I. It pursues two main objectives: a) ensuring the alignment between R&I investments and the
EU's political priorities; and b) developing strategic priorities with Member States. The pact for R&I
in the EU was adopted as a Council recommendation in 2021. It includes four dimensions:
    a set of common values and principles for R&I in the EU, which Member States and stakeholders
    should apply and promote (such as academic freedom);
    the identification of areas for joint actions among Member States and the EU, including initiatives
    to support young researchers, open science and world class scientific infrastructure;
    prioritisation of investment and reforms to ensure an appropriate level of investment;
    a framework for policy coordination and monitoring by the Member States and the EU, based on
    the ERA policy agenda approved by the Council, which sets out the joint ERA actions.

In 2023, the Council adopted a recommendation on a European framework to attract and retain
research, innovation and entrepreneurial talents. It sets out general principles and guidelines relating
to the recruitment and employment of academic researchers.

FURTHER READING
European Parliament, Zaharieva hearing documents and CV, 2024.
Evroux C., Enhancing research security, EPRS, European Parliament, March 2024.
Evroux C., The EU global approach to research and innovation, EPRS, European Parliament, March 2023.
Fay E., EP Academic Freedom Monitor 2023, EPRS, European Parliament, February 2024.

                                    Author: Rafał Mańko
                                 Members' Research Service

EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
The newly created portfolio for Democracy, Justice and Rule of Law – assigned by Commission
President-elect Ursula von der Leyen to Michael McGrath – expands the previous Justice portfolio
held by Didier Reynders. In addition to the previously included areas of rule of law, judicial
cooperation, consumer protection and data protection, it now includes democracy and
fundamental rights.
In her mission letter, von der Leyen asks McGrath to maintain dialogue with Parliament on its indirect
legislative initiatives under Article 225 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the EU (TFEU), as well as
to organise, within 100 days of taking office, the first edition of a youth policy dialogue. McGrath
will be responsible for reducing reporting burdens on businesses by at least 25 % (35 % as regards
small and medium-sized enterprises). He is tasked with organising two implementation dialogues
per year involving stakeholders, and preparing an annual implementation and enforcement report in
his areas of responsibility. In the area of democracy, McGrath will be responsible for the European
democracy shield, combating foreign information interference and disinformation, maintaining the
fairness and integrity of elections and ensuring media independence. He will also be responsible for
setting up a civil society platform to strengthen civil society, activists and human rights defenders.
In the area of fundamental rights he will be in charge of monitoring the application of the EU Charter
and EU accession to the European Convention on Human Rights (ECHR), as well as overseeing the
functioning of the European arrest warrant.
In the area of the rule of law, McGrath will be in charge of the Commission's annual rule of law
reports and is specifically tasked with adding a single market dimension to the report. He will work
with the Commissioner for Budget, Anti-Fraud and Public Administration in developing a European
anti-corruption approach and ensuring that respect for the rule of law is a precondition for obtaining
access to EU funds.
In the area of company law, he will be in charge of developing a harmonised set of rules for EU
companies (a European '28th regime' on top of the 27 legal systems of the Member States).
In the area of consumer policy, he will lead in developing the next consumer agenda 2025-2030,
and in the area of data protection he will be in charge of overseeing the enforcement of the General
Data Protection Regulation (GDPR). He will also be in charge of preparing a Digital Fairness Act to
tackle unethical digital techniques and commercial practices. Finally, he will reflect on the possible
expansion of the sphere of powers of the European Public Prosecutor's Office (EPPO).

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In a 2023 Eurobarometer survey on democracy, only 10 % of EU citizens polled were 'very satisfied' with the
way democracy worked in their country, while a further 37 % were 'somewhat satisfied', and the majority
(51 %) were not satisfied. They stated that the two main threats to democracy were false and/or misleading
information (38 %) and growing distrust and scepticism towards democratic institutions (32 %). In a 2024
Eurobarometer survey on the rule of law, the vast majority of EU citizens polled (89 %) found it important that
all Member States respect EU values, including the rule of law, and 86 % considered it important that media
and civil society organisations in all other EU Member States be able to operate freely and without pressure.
A clear majority (72 %) believed that the EU plays an important role in helping uphold the rule of law in their
country. However, a June 2024 Eurobarometer survey found that EU citizens were not fully satisfied with the
quality of judicial independence in their Member States – with as many as 37 % of them finding it bad or
even very bad. In a 2024 special Eurobarometer survey on the Digital Decade respondents specified misuse
of personal data (46 %) and fake news and disinformation (45 %) as issues having the biggest personal
impact. In the framework of the Conference on the Future of Europe (2021-2022), citizens voiced expectations
concerning the EU's role in the area of justice and the rule of law, with several contributions highlighting the
need for stronger EU action to uphold the rule of law, and calling for a high level of data protection and
stringent implementation.



2
                                                      119
                                                                          Democracy, Justice and Rule of Law


A flagship initiative in the area of democracy will be the European democracy shield aimed at
countering foreign information manipulation and interference online. In her address to the European
Parliament on 18 July 2024, von der Leyen stated that 'the Shield will take into account
recommendations from the work of the special committees on foreign interference, to better
protect our democracies'. Forthcoming challenges include the application and policing of online
content in line with the requirements of the Digital Services Act and the Artificial Intelligence Act.
Regarding the rule of law, the latest rule of law report (July 2024) revealed that many Member
States still have a lot to accomplish when it comes to ensuring the independence of the judiciary
and prosecution services. Some Member States are actually facing a deteriorating situation
concerning media freedom, checks and balances and the space for civil society. In the area of civil
law, in 2022 the Commission published a proposal for a directive on adapting non-contractual civil
liability rules to ensure an adequate level of protection for persons harmed by artificial intelligence
(AI) systems (the AI liability directive). The EPPO has yet to unleash its full potential, and there are
ongoing discussions about expanding its area of competence.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Democracy and the rule of law – two of the EU's fundamental values – are enshrined in Articles 2 and 7 of
the Treaty on European Union (TEU). The rule of law is also enshrined in Article 19 TEU as a requirement for
effective judicial protection, and in Article 47 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights as a fundamental right.
Article 6(2) TEU obliges the EU to become a party to the European Convention on Human Rights.
Article 10(1) TEU states that the EU is founded on representative democracy. Parliament has the authority to
trigger Article 7 TEU proceedings if a Member State is in breach of any one of the EU values, including the rule
of law. In the areas of civil justice and private international law (Article 81 Treaty on the Functioning of the
EU, TFEU), the ordinary legislative procedure (OLP) applies, except for legislation concerning family law
matters, where a special legislative procedure (SLP) applies. Criminal justice falls under Articles 82-86 TFEU
(OLP, with the exception of Article 86 on the EPPO – SLP, enhanced cooperation). Both in the case of criminal
procedure (Article 82) and substantive criminal law (Article 83), brake/accelerator clauses apply. EU action in
the area of data protection is based on Article 16 TFEU (OLP). Consumer protection laws, as well as certain
civil procedure laws, are adopted on the basis of Article 114 TFEU (OLP).

During its 2019-2024 term, the Parliament established two successive temporary parliamentary
committees on foreign interference in democratic processes in the EU, including disinformation. In
resolutions adopted in March 2022 and June 2023, respectively, Parliament called for a common
strategy to deal with foreign interference and disinformation campaigns, including by supporting
independent media channels, fact-checkers and researchers. Again during its previous term,
Parliament supported the Commission's efforts to uphold the rule of law and called for more action
on its part, also with regard to the primacy of EU law (resolution of 21 November 2023).
Parliament has also closely monitored the Commission's implementation of the General
Conditionality Regulation and the oversight of progress on the rule of law in the context of recovery
and resilience plans. In its most recent resolution of 18 January 2024, Parliament criticised the
Commission for unblocking Hungary's Recovery and Resilience Facility funds despite ongoing
concerns about that Member State's rule of law record. Parliament's concern for data protection
and privacy is also reflected in a range of recommendations, resolutions and mission reports. It drew
firm conclusions about the abuse of spyware in the EU, objected to the Commission's draft adequacy
decisions for the United States and the United Kingdom, and has called repeatedly for stringent
implementation of the GDPR. Parliament has also systematically commented on the annual rule of
law reports. In its recent resolution on the third rule of law report (2023), Parliament welcomed the
addition of country-specific recommendations but called once again on the Commission to expand
the scope of its reporting to cover all values enshrined in Article 2 TEU and the entire rule of law
checklist of the Council of Europe's Venice Commission. It also urged the Commission to enter into
negotiations on an interinstitutional agreement creating an EU mechanism on democracy, the rule
of law and fundamental rights.



                                                                                                              3
                                                   120
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Recent developments
The outgoing Commission's term saw numerous initiatives in various areas covered by this portfolio.
In the area of media freedom, these initiatives included the adoption of the anti-SLAPP Directive to
protect journalists and activists from abusive litigation, and the European Media Freedom Act to
safeguard EU journalists and media from political or economic interference. In the area of justice
cooperation, initiatives include the adoption of rules on the e-CODEX platform and on digitalisation
of judicial cooperation. The area of criminal justice was reinforced through the launch of the
European Public Prosecutor's Office (EPPO).
In the area of digital policies, the EU legislature adopted the Digital Services Act, which imposes
asymmetric obligations on very large online platforms and search engines, particularly regarding the
removal of illegal online content related to foreign interference, misinformation or content harmful
to minors. Adopted in 2024, the Artificial Intelligence Act (AIA) lays down harmonised rules on
artificial intelligence (AI), including clear requirements and obligations for specific uses of AI. One
of the AIA's objectives is to enable users to identify AI-generated content that includes deep fake
audio and video, which must be labelled as such.
Regarding data protection, the Commission tabled a proposal on GDPR procedural rules to
streamline the handling of cross-border cases across the EU. In consumer law, the Commission
adopted a report on the implementation of the directive on the better enforcement and
modernisation of Union consumer protection rules. Additionally, work is ongoing on a review of the
Consumer Protection Cooperation Regulation. A report on the outcome of the fitness check of EU
consumer law in the context of digital fairness, published on 3 October 2024, focuses on directives
related to unfair commercial practices, consumer rights and unfair terms.
Finally, technical negotiations on EU accession to the ECHR were finalised in March 2023.

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                       Author: Monika Kiss
                                   Members' Research Service
                                  PE 762.446 – September 2024                                          EN
                                                     122
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
Transport enables the free movement of people across the EU and beyond, and contributes to
cohesion and competitiveness within the internal market. It accounts for about 5 % of the EU's gross
domestic product (GDP) and therefore has an important influence on economic growth and
employment. Some 10.2 million people are directly employed in the sector. Transport emissions,
currently at around 25 % of total EU greenhouse gas emissions, are on the increase.
Dating back to the Treaty of Rome, European transport policy has evolved significantly over time,
expanding to encompass various new aspects such as technical advancements, fuel sources,
transport networks and funding. While transport policy is one of the EU's first common policy areas,
the implementation of EU legislation and concrete measures in the Member States lies with the
competent national, regional and local authorities. A milestone in EU transport policy was the 2011
strategy, seeking to develop a single European transport area, reduce the EU's dependence on
imported fossil fuels, cut CO 2 emissions in transport by 60 % by 2050 and modernise the transport
system. However, these objectives fell short of the targets set during the December 2015 Climate
Conference in Paris (COP21). As a result, the Commission adopted an action plan – the European
Green Deal. In December 2020, the Commission published its sustainable and smart mobility
strategy, designed to make the EU's transport system more sustainable, digital and resilient, in line
with the European Green Deal and the EU's digital strategy. In December 2022, the Council outlined
the European agenda for tourism 2030, a comprehensive programme covering the green and digital
transition, resilience, inclusion, skills and support, the relevant policy framework and governance.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
A Eurobarometer survey on passenger rights, published in July 2024, shows that the use of transport services
in the EU has decreased slightly compared with 2019 figures (73 % versus 74 %), especially in air transport
(-4 %) and road transport (-2 %). Awareness of passenger rights is most widespread for rail transport (33 %),
followed by air transport (30 %). Some 47 % of rail passengers had experienced at least one disruption in their
journey during the previous 12 months, mostly due to departure delays; this was 9 % more than in 2019. For air
travellers, these disruptions account for 30 % of their journeys, 2 % more than in 2019.
In a survey on the digitalisation of travel documents, published in September 2023, 68 % of EU citizens polled
were in favour of using digital travel documents for extra-Schengen travel, and 66 % would prefer to use a
single application for digital travel documents at EU level, rather than one for each Member State.

In her political guidelines for the new Commission, President-elect Ursula von der Leyen stated that
the new Commission will prioritise investment in clean energy infrastructure and technologies,
including transport infrastructure and the deployment of a hydrogen network. Another focus will be
a strengthened cohesion and growth policy, especially in remote regions. The success of this policy
will depend greatly on reforms and investment in transport and digital connectivity. Through the
Global Gateway initiative, the EU intends to invest in transport corridors, ports, renewable energy
generation and green hydrogen production to help developing countries.
The European Council's 2024-2029 strategic agenda states that, on the path towards climate
neutrality by 2050, enhanced electrification using all net-zero- and low-carbon solutions, and
investment in grids, storage and interconnections are required, especially in transport.
According to the mission letter outlining his responsibilities, Tzitzikostas will have to ensure that the
missing parts of the trans-European transport network (TEN-T) are built by 2030, along with the
swift deployment of charging infrastructure with harmonised charging fees and payment methods.
The Commission is to submit plans for a high-speed rail network connecting EU Member State
capitals and a comprehensive EU port strategy. Moreover, Tzitzikostas will have to ensure that the
EU transport system is further digitalised through the deployment of intelligent transport systems,
smart mobility solutions and the European rail traffic management system (ERTMS). To this end,




2
                                                      123
                                                                                          Transport and Tourism


strong transport connections have to be made with neighbouring regions, and a comprehensive
dialogue has to be started to address inefficiencies within the Single European Sky. By the end of
2025, the Commission is required to publish a first progress report on the European declaration on
cycling. Transport security, including cybersecurity, needs to be reinforced, necessitating
cooperation with the Commissioner for Defence to ensure smooth continent-wide military mobility.
Decarbonisation efforts should be enhanced through a sustainable transport investment plan and
proposals for clean corporate fleets. The single market for transport services has to be
strengthened, and there is a need for a regulation on single digital booking and ticketing, allowing
the use of one ticket during a multimodal trip. The EU automotive sector needs an industrial action
plan, and the maritime manufacturing sector needs a new industrial strategy. Regarding future
means of transportation, the use of cutting-edge technologies, such as Hyperloop, should be
explored. The social dimension of transport – including transport poverty, access in rural areas, as
well as skilled worker shortages – should also be addressed. Finally, a resilient and competitive
tourism sector should be developed, in line with the EU agenda for tourism 2030.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Article 4(2)(g) (Title I) and Articles 90 to 100 (Title VI) of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union
cover transport; and Article 4(2)(h) (Title I) and Articles 170 to 172 (Title XVI) deal with trans-European
networks (TEN-T). The EU shares competence with the Member States in the area of transport and the TEN-T.
As the ordinary legislative procedure applies to both areas, the Council and Parliament act as co-legislators.
Parliament was a co-legislator for the European Climate Law and the 'fit for 55' initiative. During the
legislative process relating to the Green Deal, such as on the Alternative Fuels Infrastructure (AFIR)
and RefuelEU Aviation Regulations, Parliament succeeded in raising the Commission's initial targets.
Parliament has influenced policymaking on transport through several own-initiative resolutions. In
its 2020 resolution on the Green Deal, Parliament called for a more comprehensive urban mobility
plan to reduce congestion and improve liveability in towns and cities. This included support for
zero-emission public transport, and cycling and walking infrastructure. In May 2023, Parliament's
own-initiative resolution on urban mobility urged Member States to develop safe, accessible,
affordable, smart, resilient and sustainable urban transport systems. It also called for their financial,
fiscal and regulatory support to help transport providers and users transition to these systems.
Parliament also played an important role in promoting healthy and green transport options. On
31 January 2023, Parliament adopted its first resolution dedicated exclusively to cycling, calling on
the Commission to develop an EU cycling strategy to maximise the potential of cycling as a
full-fledged transport mode. This call was successful, and on 2 April 2024 the Commission, the
Council and Parliament signed the European declaration on cycling.

Recent developments
To meet the Green Deal goal of making Europe the first climate-neutral continent by 2050,
transport-related emissions must be cut by 90 % by 2050. This can be achieved by replacing fossil
fuels with alternative fuels such as electricity or biofuels and by changing habits and transitioning to
greener mobility alternatives. The Alternative Fuels Infrastructure Regulation (AFIR) sets mandatory
targets for the deployment of alternative fuels infrastructure (electric charging and hydrogen
refuelling stations) in the EU, for road vehicles, vessels and stationary aircraft. The RefuelEU Aviation
Directive sets minimum obligations for fuel suppliers to gradually increase the share of advanced
biofuels and synthetic aviation fuels in the fuel supplied to operators at EU airports, following a set
timetable. Additionally, the FuelEU Maritime Regulation imposes increasingly strict limits on the
carbon intensity of energy used by vessels starting in 2025, requiring them to use alternative fuels.
The sustainable and smart mobility strategy and action plan lay the foundations to enable the EU
transport system to achieve its green and digital transformation and become more resilient to future



                                                                                                                3
                                                   124
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


crises. The action plan lists 10 flagships and 82 initiatives, including revision of the Trans-European
Transport Network (TEN-T) Regulation, with the ultimate goal of closing gaps, removing bottlenecks
and technical barriers, and strengthening social, economic and territorial cohesion in the EU. The
reshaped TEN-T network will serve both military and civilian purposes. The 2021-2027 multiannual
EU budget extended the operation of the Connecting Europe Facility (CEF) – the EU's funding
programme supporting the development of transport, energy, and digital infrastructure across
trans-European networks. Recently adopted, the new Intelligent Transport Systems (ITS) Directive
aims to establish interoperable and seamless ITS services across Member States, adapting them to
new road mobility options, mobility apps and connected and automated mobility. The proposal for
the revision of the Single European Sky (SES2+ package) is close to final adoption. Its aim is to
reform the organisation and performance of air traffic control in the EU, focusing on air traffic
management and air navigation services, to meet future capacity and safety requirements.
In line with the Vision Zero objective, which seeks to minimise fatalities in road transport by 2050,
the Commission has put forward a road safety package consisting of three proposals: a directive on
cross-border enforcement of traffic rules, a revision of the Directive on Driving Licences and a
directive on EU-wide disqualification for major traffic offences. While the first is close to adoption,
the legislative procedure is still ongoing for the latter two.
Work is ongoing on a number of legislative procedures initiated during the 2019-2024 term, such as
the maritime safety package. This package includes legislation on flag State inspections, port State
control, maritime accident investigations, ship-source pollution and the revision of the mandate of
the European Maritime Safety Agency (EMSA). Legislative efforts will also continue to modernise
river information services (RIS). The greening of freight package, work on which is also ongoing,
proposes measures to enhance the efficiency and sustainability of freight transport. This includes
improving rail infrastructure management, providing stronger incentives for low-emission trucks,
and offering better information on greenhouse gas emissions from freight transport.

Christophe Hansen was re-elected as a Member of the European Parliament in June 2024 where he

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                    Author: Claudia Vinci
