EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Fundamentals of the portfolio
The agriculture and food portfolio covers a number of areas: EU policies and funding arrangements
for farming support; rural development; and agricultural market measures under the common
agricultural policy (CAP). The disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic from 2020 and Russia's
invasion of Ukraine from 2022 highlighted the importance of a resilient EU agrifood sector and
contributed to shaping the 2023-2027 CAP, in force since 1 January 2023. This legal framework,
along with the EU Member States' national CAP strategic plans, is designed to help achieve the goals
of the European Green Deal and the 'farm to fork' and biodiversity strategies by 2030.
CAP strategic plans are structured around the CAP's 10 key objectives and must include targets,
conditions for interventions and financial resources aligned with each specific objective and
identified needs.
In January 2024, as announced earlier by the European Commission, the strategic dialogue on EU
agriculture forum was launched amid farmer protests across the EU. It came up with a number of
recommendations, including promoting generational renewal, supporting organic production and
enhancing food security. Additionally, the forum recommended that the future Commissioner for
Agriculture and Food develop a 'vision for agriculture and food', to be presented within the first
100 days of the new Commission.

Priorities and challenges
    Public opinion
    Responding to the 2022 special Eurobarometer survey on Europeans, agriculture and the CAP:
        95 % of survey participants believed that agriculture and rural areas were important for the future
          of the EU;
        67 % of participants thought that EU farmers should alter their practices to address climate
          change;
        50 % of respondents agreed that one of the main responsibilities of farmers was to provide safe,
          healthy and sustainable food of high quality;
        49 % of respondents agreed that securing a stable supply of food in the EU at all times should be
          among the CAP's key objectives;
        76 % of respondents considered that the CAP benefited all EU citizens and not only farmers.
    Source: Special Eurobarometer 520, European Commission, February-March 2022.


In its July 2024 strategic agenda 2024-2029, the European Council defined the EU's priorities for the
new institutional cycle. The document emphasises the need to establish a 'competitive, sustainable
and resilient agricultural sector that continues to ensure food security'. This includes reinforcing the
position of farmers in the food supply chain, protecting nature, and reversing the degradation of
ecosystems. Finally, the document calls for action to strengthen water resilience across the EU.

In her July 2024 political guidelines for the new Commission, President-elect Ursula von der Leyen
stated that the EU produces the healthiest and highest-quality food in the world thanks to its nine
million farms and age-old agricultural tradition. She highlighted the importance of EU agriculture for
food security in the face of increasing pressure from factors such as climate change, unfair global
competition and mounting energy prices. She also noted the constraints, among them a shortage of
younger farmers and difficulties in accessing capital. The Commission President-elect committed to
enhancing the sector's competitiveness through investment, support for farms, cooperatives and
SMEs, as well as protecting farmers against unfair trading practices. The September 2024 report by
Mario Draghi on The future of European competitiveness focuses on sectors other than agriculture,
despite the CAP having a 31 % share in the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework. The report



2
                                                       127
                                                                                            Agriculture and Food


does, however, recognise the CAP as an EU strategic priority, and recommends a complete
reorganisation of agricultural and cohesion subsidies to make room for other EU funding more
targeted at innovation and skills.
In her mission letter to Hansen, von der Leyen outlined the key priorities for his portfolio: to
strengthen the competitiveness, resilience and sustainability of the agricultural sector. To achieve
this, he needs to follow up on the strategic dialogue on EU agriculture report and recommendations,
and to deliver a 'vision for agriculture and food' within his first 100 days. As part of his portfolio, he
will have to ensure that adequate support is provided for farmers in need, that the CAP is 'fit for
purpose', provides 'targeted support' for farmers and is balanced, and that family farms and young
farmers have easier access to capital for generational renewal.
Farmers must be guaranteed a fair and sufficient income, but also protection against unfair trading
practices. The focus of Hansen's portfolio will also be on sustainability and decarbonisation. This will
involve supporting organic farming and contributing to the climate adaptation plan. Hansen will work
under the guidance of the Commission Executive Vice President for Cohesion Policy, Regional
Development and Cities to implement an EU-wide benchmarking system in the agri-food sector,
and to harmonise practices and direct comparisons across different sustainability objectives and
ambitions. Lastly, Hansen will be expected to cooperate with the Commissioner for Enlargement
and other stakeholders to contribute to a review of pre-enlargement policy.

European Parliament
Legal basis for the EU's and European Parliament's competence in the area of agriculture
Article 4(2) of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU) states that the EU shares
competence with Member States in the area of agriculture. The main legal basis of EU agricultural policy is set
out in Title III TFEU, specifically in Articles 38 to 44, which spell out the main objectives of the CAP: i) to
increase agricultural productivity; ii) to ensure a fair standard of living for farmers; iii) to stabilise markets;
iv) to guarantee food supplies; v) and to ensure that supplies are accessible to consumers at reasonable prices.
The Lisbon Treaty gave Parliament the power to co-legislate in most agricultural policy matters.

During its ninth term, Parliament worked intensively on a number of significant agriculture-related
legislative files, among them:
     files of relevance to the CAP:
           o common organisation of the markets in agricultural products;
           o financing, management and monitoring of the CAP (2021–2027);
           o support for the strategic plans to be drawn up by Member States;
           o good agricultural and environmental condition standards; schemes for
                climate, environment and animal welfare; amendment of the CAP strategic
                plans; review of the CAP strategic plans; and exemptions from controls and
                penalties.
     resolutions on:
           o generational renewal in the EU farms of the future;
           o ensuring food security and long-term resilience of the EU agriculture;
           o EU action plan for organic agriculture;
           o long-term vision for the EU's rural areas;
           o EU strategy on adaptation to climate change;
           o need for an urgent EU action plan to ensure food security inside and outside
                the EU in light of the Russian invasion of Ukraine;
Parliament's Committee on Agriculture and Rural Development (AGRI) is responsible for reviewing
and, if necessary, modifying the Commission's legislative proposals on agricultural policy. It drafts
reports that are later voted by Parliament in plenary. It also oversees the functioning of various
financial instruments and legislation on veterinary and plant health, animal feed, animal welfare (now
to be under the responsibility of the Commissioner for Health and Animal Welfare), quality of



                                                                                                                 3
                                                   128
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


agricultural products, forestry, and agroforestry. AGRI also exercises scrutiny by engaging in regular
dialogue with the relevant Commissioner on issues such as unexpected market disruptions faced by
EU producers, and pest outbreaks.

Recent developments
During Parliament's 2019-2024 term, EU agriculture was affected by many dramatic events,
including the COVID-19 pandemic, Russia's invasion of Ukraine, and the farmer protests in the
run-up to the June 2024 European Parliament elections.
During that term, the Parliament adopted the legislative proposals for the 2023-2027 CAP, which
entered into force on 1 January 2023. Key components of the revised CAP were presented in a series
of documents published between 2019 and 2022. These documents highlight the importance of
enhancing biodiversity and complying with the EU's environmental and climate laws. Notably,
Member States must ensure that at least 35 % of the rural development budget and at least 25 % of
direct payments are dedicated to environmental and climate measures.
Following widespread demonstrations by farmers opposing the CAP's administrative complexities
and stringent environmental rules and conditions, the Commission put forward a proposal aimed at
reducing the numerous environmental obligations farmers had to fulfil in order to qualify for direct
payments. Additionally, the proposal envisaged removing controls and penalties relating to
environmental requirements for small farms, and simplifying the process for Member States to
modify their national CAP strategic plans. The opening of negotiations for Ukraine's accession, which
Parliament welcomed in a February 2024 resolution, could have an impact on the CAP's future
design. This may require adaptation of EU policies and budgetary resources to accommodate
Ukraine's significant agricultural production. Additionally, following the Commission's report on the
implementation of the Directive on Unfair trading practices, published in April 2024, some changes
outside the CAP are expected to make agricultural enterprises more financially sustainable.

Fundamentals of the portfolio
According to the mission letter outlining the contents of his portfolio, Glenn Micallef will be
responsible for supporting young people and building on commonalities that bring people together,
from cultural heritage to sport. Micallef will therefore have to develop a strategy on
intergenerational fairness to map out how to strengthen communication between generations, and
to make sure that decisions taken today do no harm to future generations. This approach is widely
endorsed by European youth organisations and civil society.
The 2019-2027 EU youth strategy (EUYS) serves as the framework for EU youth policy cooperation
and aims to foster the participation of young people in democratic life. The EU youth dialogue takes
place within the framework of the youth strategy and has the goal of ensuring that the views and
needs of young people are taken into account in the EU's youth policies.
Culture and sport are policy areas deeply intertwined with the daily lives of many EU citizens, and
are a key component of shared European identity and values. When it comes to culture, the EU is
responsible for a variety of tasks, including safeguarding and promoting cultural and linguistic
diversity, advancing cultural exchanges and supporting artists' mobility. The creative and cultural
industries (CCIs) play a crucial role in promoting EU cultural diversity. The Creative Europe
programme supports the cultural and creative sectors (CCSs) through cooperation projects that
connect artists across the EU. EU sport-related initiatives promote sport's role in health and
wellbeing, social inclusion and cohesion, sustainability and equality. The Erasmus+ programme is key
not only in advancing youth policy cooperation but also in promoting sport's European dimension.

Priorities and challenges
Expectations/public opinion
In a Eurobarometer survey on youth and democracy published in May 2024, 64 % of the young people taking
part planned to vote in the upcoming European elections. However, 19 % said they were not interested in
politics and 13 % said they were not interested in voting. To get their voices heard by decision-makers, 38 %
of respondents believed the most effective way was by voting, while 32 % believed it was by 'engaging in social
media'. Almost half (49 %) of respondents reported having taken action to change society, such as signing a
petition, participating in a rally, or sending a letter to a politician, in the past year.
Social media play a central role in young people's lives, with the vast majority using them daily and almost
constantly throughout the day. According to Eurostat, in 2023, 83 % of young people aged 16 to 29 in the EU
used social media networks, and 97 % used the internet on a daily basis. While social media can have many
positive uses, such as fostering connectedness, helping job searches and communicating about emergencies,
their pervasiveness also raises concerns about adverse effects. Young people are routinely exposed to
harmful, illegal or age-inappropriate content on social media, such as hate messages, violent or pornographic
images, pro-anorexic content and dis- and misinformation. They also face the risk of becoming victims of
cyberbullying or hidden marketing practices. According to the EU Kids online survey, about one in ten children
becomes a victim of online bullying each month.

Key actions in the youth sector in the coming years will include giving young people a stronger voice
in EU policymaking, introducing the newly announced youth check – a youth impact assessment of
EU legislation – and specifying how it will operate in practice. Furthermore, a youth stakeholders'
platform will be launched, facilitating exchange with youth organisations and youth researchers.
Additionally, a (Commission) President's youth advisory board will be established, composed of
young people from all Member States who will advise the Commission President, Ursula von der
Leyen, on issues that matter to them. Glenn Micallef has been assigned the specific task of
coordinating the annual youth policy dialogues organised by each Commissioner. The first such
dialogues have to take place within the first 100 days of the Commission's new term.
To protect the mental health of children and young people online, Micallef will have to focus on the
impact of social media and excessive screen time on young people. An EU-wide inquiry into the
effects of social media on well-being will be launched, together with actions to address the addictive



2
                                                      131
                                                            Intergenerational Fairness, Youth, Culture and Sport


design of online services. Micallef will lead the work on developing an action plan against
cyberbullying and implementing the EU strategy on the rights of the child, aimed at integrating
children's rights into all relevant EU areas of work.
The Commission is required to carry out an interim evaluation of the 2021-2027 Erasmus+
programme and a final evaluation of the 2014-2020 programme by the end of 2024. Similarly, the
mid-term evaluation of the 2021-2027 European Solidarity Corps programme and the final
evaluation of the 2018-2020 programme will be ongoing throughout 2024.
Micallef should also contribute to the development of an artificial intelligence (AI) strategy for
cultural and creative industries and of a new strategic framework, the culture compass. Improving
the working conditions of artists and cultural professionals is a key component of this framework.
EU support for building more crisis-resilient creative and cultural sectors will continue, along with
support for their green and digital transition through funding from the Creative Europe programme.
The Commission is expected to complete its evaluation of the current and former Creative Europe
programmes by the end of 2024.
EU action in sport policy, which is guided by the EU's 3-year work plans for sport (the current one
covering the 2024-2027 period), should be aimed at: strengthening the EU sport model through a
new strategy; promoting fairness, openness, and the integrity of sportspeople; fostering sport
diplomacy and cooperation with partners around the world; and updating the Council
recommendation on health-enhancing physical activity.

European Parliament
Treaty basis and European Parliament competence
Policies in the area of youth, culture and sport are essentially made and implemented by the Member States
(Articles 165 and 167 Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU). The EU's role is one of support
and coordination, with harmonisation of laws and regulations being specifically excluded. Respect of linguistic
diversity, protection of cultural heritage as well as promotion of solidarity between generations and protection
of the rights of the child are enshrined in Article 3 of the Treaty on European Union (TEU).

Parliament succeeded in obtaining an increase in the funding for the 2021-2027 Creative Europe and
Erasmus+ programmes. In its resolution on the European Year of Youth 2022, Parliament committed
to ensuring that the year leaves a strong and lasting legacy by integrating youth policy into all
relevant EU policy areas. Parliament welcomed the European solidarity corps programme and the
extension of its activities to include humanitarian aid operations. Parliament also considered the
impact of COVID-19 closures on children and young people. In its 2023 resolution, Parliament called
for an evaluation of the addictive and mental health effects of social media platforms and a review
of any existing EU legislation if necessary.
Over the years, Parliament has played a crucial role in pushing children's rights to the core of EU
policies, particularly through its Intergroup on Children Rights and the Parliament's Coordinator on
Children's Rights, a post that has been held by Parliament's Vice-President Ewa Kopacz since 2019.
As a follow-up to its resolution on the situation of artists and the cultural recovery in the EU,
Parliament requested the Commission to propose legislative instruments, among them a directive,
to establish an EU framework addressing the social and professional situation of artists and other
CCS professionals. Parliament has also shown support for the New European Bauhaus initiative and
the EU book sector, and highlighted challenges faced by authors in the EU music streaming market.
Furthermore, Parliament has examined the impacts of AI technologies on education, culture and the
audiovisual sector, and underlined the need to clarify the conditions for using copyright-protected
content as data input, and explored issues related to the EU's media landscape in the digital decade.
Central to Parliament's sport-related work was a comprehensive own-initiative report on EU sports
policy. In the past term, Parliament also adopted a resolution on e-sports and videogames.




                                                                                                              3
                                                    132
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Recent developments
The Commission presented the EU strategy on the rights of the child in March 2021. The 2022
European Year of Youth (EYY) aimed to boost young people's involvement in the EU's democratic
life and mainstream youth policy across all relevant EU policies. In 2024, two essential documents
shaping EU youth policy were a Commission communication on the EYY, announcing the
introduction of the above-mentioned youth check, and a report on the interim evaluation of the
2019-2027 EUYS. The 10th EU Youth Dialogue cycle started in July 2023. The same year, a New
European Bauhaus Route was added to the DiscoverEU travel route suggestions.
The Commission's better internet for kids strategy (BIK+), adopted in 2022, outlines specific actions
that the Commission plans to support, including the EU code of conduct on age-appropriate design
and helplines to assist in addressing harmful content, contact or conduct. Various EU laws also
provide support. For example, the Digital Services Act requires online platforms to promptly remove
illegal content (such as revenge porn) and prohibits the use of minors' personal data for targeted
advertising. However, a lot of work remains to be done.
The EU work plan for culture 2023-2026 is the current framework for EU-level cultural policy
cooperation covering various aspects, including the working conditions of artists and cultural
professionals, as well as the green transition of the CCSs. In 2022, a Commission report on the
cultural dimension of sustainable development in EU actions and an EU action plan against
trafficking in cultural goods were adopted. After a setback from the COVID-19 pandemic, the
European Capitals of Culture programme fully resumed in 2022. The 2021-2027 Creative Europe
programme was launched with a budget of €2.44 billion, a major increase compared to the previous
period. Additionally, the Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) allocates about €11 billion to the
CCSs.
In 2023, an Erasmus+ mobility action for sports staff became available for the first time. In February
2024, the Commission published an implementation report on the fourth EU work plan for sport. The
current such work plan, for 2024-2027 sets three priorities: protecting integrity and values in sport;
addressing socio-economic and environmental dimensions in sport; and promoting participation in
sport and health-enhancing physical activity.

BRIEFING



              Confirmation hearings of the
               Commissioners-designate
         A decisive step in the investiture process
SUMMARY
The confirmation hearings of the Commissioners-designate before the European Parliament's
committees play a vital role in informing Parliament before it decides on the proposed composition
of the College of Commissioners. Ahead of the autumn 2024 hearings, Parliament has adapted the
rules for the process, which will apply for the members of the second von der Leyen College.
First, the Legal Affairs Committee scrutinises the declaration of interests of each Commissioner-
designate. Second, each Commissioner-designate appears before the competent parliamentary
committee or committees for a single confirmation hearing.
In the past, the main criticism levelled at some of the Commissioners-designate has involved their
having insufficient expertise in their respective portfolios, as well as the vagueness of their answers
and their reluctance to make political commitments. The existence of possible conflicts of interest
in relation to the assigned portfolio and concerns regarding the integrity of the candidate have
influenced the dynamics of more recent hearings. Since the 2004 investiture, Parliament has used
its role in appointing the Commission to replace certain controversial candidates and force
adjustments to certain portfolios, although according to the Treaties Parliament can only reject or
accept the College as a whole.
While some experts have advised against the excessive politicisation of the confirmation hearings,
others have welcomed the increased accountability of the Commission to Parliament, and have
praised the deepening of the political link between the two institutions as a step towards further
democratisation of the EU decision-making process. Confirmation hearings have become critical for
Parliament to hold the Commission accountable, and have gained prominence as a tool for
Parliament to take a greater role in EU agenda-setting.
This is a further updated version of a September 2019 briefing by Maria Diaz Crego, itself an update
of a 2014 briefing by Eva-Maria Poptcheva.

                 EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                                     Author: Kamil Baraník
                                  Members' Research Service
                                  PE 762.400 – October 2024                                               EN

EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Parliament's role in the appointment of Commissioners
Ever-increasing influence
Commissioners were originally appointed by common accord of the Member States' governments
for a renewable term of office of four years (Article 158 Treaty of Rome). The Commission was then
composed of nine members, with small Member States appointing one Commissioner and large
Member States (France, Germany and Italy) two. The founding Treaties did not involve the European
Parliament in the procedure leading to the appointment of the Commission, although Commission
Presidents used to deliver a general policy statement before Parliament when taking office. Once
the Commission was in office, Parliament could adopt a motion of censure of the entire College.
Parliament pushed to be involved in the investiture procedure in the 1970s and 1980s, to enhance
the democratic legitimacy of the Commission. Although not required by the Treaties, Parliament's
efforts led to the introduction of a vote of confidence in the designated Commission as early as 1981.
However, it was not until 1993, with the Maastricht Treaty, that Parliament formally gained a role in
the appointment process. The Treaty aligned Parliament's and the Commission's terms of office (five
years) and provided for Parliament to be consulted by Member States' governments before
nominating the President of the Commission. Similarly, the Treaty required the President and
Commissioners to be subject as a body to a vote of approval by Parliament (Article 158 Treaty on
European Union, TEU). Parliament adapted its Rules of Procedure to require the nominated President
to make a statement, followed by a debate, in Parliament (Rule 29) and for the nominated
Commissioners to appear before parliamentary committees (Rule 29a) before the vote of approval.
The first Commission subject to such 'hearings' was the Santer Commission in 1995, although
President Jacques Santer accepted the new procedure only after receiving assurances from
Parliament's President that there would be no parliamentary vote on individual Commissioners-
designate. 1 The US Congress's 'confirmation hearings' dealing with the presidential nominations to
judicial and executive posts served as an inspiration for this procedure.2
The Amsterdam and Nice Treaties introduced further modifications to the composition of the
Commission and the appointment procedure. With a view to the future eastern enlargement of the
Union, the Treaty of Nice provided for a reduction in the number of Commissioners, through the
adoption of the principle of 'one Commissioner per Member State' (Article 4, Protocol on the
enlargement of the European Union). The five largest Member States (France, Germany, Italy, the
United Kingdom and Spain) thus lost their second Commissioner, a provision that was first applied
in 2004 to the first Barroso Commission.
For the appointment procedure, the Amsterdam Treaty gave Parliament a greater role in relation to
the election of the Commission President, holding a separate vote of approval (not only being
consulted) on the candidate nominated by the Member States (Article 214 Treaty of Amsterdam).
Similarly, as from the Treaty of Amsterdam, Commissioners-designate would have to be nominated
by the Member States by common accord with the President-elect of the Commission (and not only
in consultation with them), a change that gave the President-elect of the Commission the formal
power to veto the list of Commissioners. The Treaty of Nice involved the Council in the procedure
as the institution formally in charge of nominating both the President (in this case, in the composition
of Heads of State or Government) and the Commissioners by qualified majority. A consensus among
all Member States was no longer required by the Treaties to nominate the candidate for the
Presidency (Article 214 Treaty of Nice), although it has remained the rule in practice. However, in
2014 Jean-Claude Juncker was nominated by qualified majority, with two Member States voting
against him.




2
                                                135
                                                 Confirmation hearings of the Commissioners-designate


The current situation
After the entry into force of the Lisbon Treaty, the link between the appointment of the Commission
and the European elections and, therefore, the 'parliamentarisation'3 of the process leading to the
appointment of the Commission, became more explicit, with the Treaties obliging the European
Council to 'take into account the elections to the European Parliament' when proposing a candidate
for the Presidency of the Commission. Moreover, they confer on Parliament the power to 'elect' (and
not only to approve) the Commission President. This modification paved the way for the
Spitzenkandidaten process, which led to the election of Jean-Claude Juncker in 2014. After the 2019
European elections, however, none of the Spitzenkandidaten were successful, with Ursula von der
Leyen eventually elected as Commission President in July 2019. Nevertheless, it seems that the
Spitzenkandidaten process became relevant again when Ursula von der Leyen, as the European
People's Party's leading candidate in the 2024 European Parliament election, was re-elected as
Commission President in July 2024. For the other members of the College, the Lisbon Treaty
introduced several changes relating to the composition of the College, the criteria for selection and
the appointment procedure.

Composition of the College: Number of Commissioners
The growing number of Commissioners (from nine to the current 27), caused by successive EU
enlargements and the decision to stick to 'one Commissioner per Member State', has led to concerns
relating to the Commission's capacity to act and adopt decisions as a collegiate body, to ensure
coordination among Commissioners and to avoid overlaps in competences and inefficiencies. 4 The
Lisbon Treaty envisaged a Commission that would consist of a number of members corresponding
to two-thirds of the number of Member States as from November 2014, unless the European Council
decided otherwise through a unanimous decision (Article 17(5) TEU). The composition of the
Commission would have to guarantee equal rotation among nationals of the different Member
States, and reflect the demographic and geographical range of all Member States, as required by
Articles 17(5) TEU and 244 TFEU. However, this provision has not yet been applied, as the European
Council decided in 2013 to maintain one Commissioner per Member State. This decision still stands
and the number of Commissioners continues to be equal to the number of Member States.

Criteria to be fulfilled by Commissioners-designate
The Lisbon Treaty also introduced changes in relation to the criteria to be used when selecting the
members of the Commission. The Commissioners are to be selected taking into account their
general competence and independence, but also their European commitment, a criterion introduced
in the Lisbon Treaty. Independence, including from national governments, is a requirement clearly
linked to the characterisation of the Commission as the EU institution promoting the general (and
not national) interest of the Union (Article 17(3) TEU). Despite this encouraged image of
independence, some authors suggest that Member States try to influence the Commission through
the appointment of loyal Commissioners (i.e. persons belonging to the government party) or
through nominations that would guarantee them an important portfolio for their Commissioner (i.e.
nominations of senior or high-profile politicians). 5 Parliament's Rules of Procedure include other
criteria that have to be taken into account when evaluating the future Commissioners – specific
knowledge of the portfolio, communication skills and gender balance (Annex VII).

Nominating the Commissioners: Who does what
The Lisbon Treaty prescribes two appointment procedures for the nominations of Commissioners
and the High Representative of the Union for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy, who is also a Vice-
President of the Commission (Article 17(5) TEU). The High Representative is appointed by the
European Council, acting by a qualified majority, with the agreement of the Commission President
(Article 18(1) TEU), whereas all the other Commissioners are proposed by the Member States'
governments and nominated by the Council in common accord with the President-elect of the




                                                                                                   3
                                                 136
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Commission (Article 17(7) TEU). The composition of the Commission therefore does not usually
reflect the political balance in the European Parliament but rather that among national governments,
with pan-European party politics playing a minor role in the selection of individual Commissioners-
designate.6
This feature clearly distinguishes the EU's institutional framework from standard parliamentary
democracies, where the composition of the government tends to represent the majority in
Parliament, to whom the national executive is also collectively responsible. However, with
Parliament's role in the procedure strengthened, a clear majority in Parliament could in future try to
put pressure on the Council to take the results of European elections more into account when
nominating Commissioners. Similarly, the role of the President-elect of the Commission in the
nomination process seems to be evolving: while it is true that the President-elect cannot shape the
College in the same way as prime ministers usually do in parliamentary democracies, their current
power to veto future Commissioners and to attribute responsibilities within the Commission has
allowed Presidents-elect to lobby for specific candidates or for certain profiles.7
On 28 June 2024, the European Council decided to propose Ursula von der Leyen as candidate for
Commission President and Kaja Kallas as High Representative. On 18 July, the European Parliament
re-elected Ursula von der Leyen as President of the European Commission (401 MEPs voted in
favour, 284 against, and 22 cast blank or invalid votes). Before the vote, Ursula von der Leyen
delivered a statement and presented the political guidelines for the next European Commission
(2024-2029). Among other things, the political guidelines specified the portfolios for some
Commissioners – it mentioned a Vice-President for Implementation, Simplification and
Interinstitutional Relations, a Commissioner for Defence, a Commissioner whose responsibilities will
include housing, a Commissioner whose responsibilities will include ensuring intergenerational
fairness, a Commissioner for Equality, a Fisheries and Oceans Commissioner, a Commissioner for
Enlargement, and a Commissioner for the Mediterranean.
In line with the post-vote statement of the Commission President-elect, she sent formal requests
to the Member States inviting them to put forward candidates for European Commissioners. With
the exception of those candidates proposed for another term in the College of Commissioners, the
Member States were asked to propose one woman and one man to allow the President-elect to
select the best candidates, and to ensure gender parity in the College. The President-elect aimed to
interview the candidates starting in mid-August. Subsequently, the Council, in agreement with the
President-elect, should adopt the list of candidates. The President-elect allocates the
responsibilities for specific areas (portfolios) to each Commissioner-designate, then Parliament calls
the candidates to the confirmation hearings.

The final steps of the procedure: Parliament's vote of consent
Once the candidates are nominated, the Commission needs to obtain the consent of Parliament by
a majority of the votes cast (Rule 129(7)) before being appointed by the European Council.
Confirmation hearings are not stipulated in the Treaties, but are required under Parliament's Rules
of Procedure (Rule 129 and Annex VII). They are a vital counterpart to the requirement to obtain
Parliament's consent, which would have no substance if Parliament could not examine the aptitude
of the candidates.
According to the Treaties, once Parliament has elected the Commission President by a majority of
its component Members, it can only refuse or accept the Commission in its entirety and not
individual Commissioners-designate – just as many parliaments in parliamentary democracies
cannot vote on individual members of government other than the prime minister. This is a
consequence of the collective responsibility of the Commission as a collegiate body. However, the
threat to vote down the designated Commission has proven to be a powerful means of encouraging
national governments to replace Commissioners-designate opposed by Parliament, and some see
this as a step towards Commissioners having individual responsibility to Parliament, a trend that
would ultimately test the collegial character of the Commission. 8



4
                                                 137
                                                  Confirmation hearings of the Commissioners-designate


Procedure for the confirmation hearings
The procedure for holding confirmation hearings is set out in Rule 129 of Parliament's Rules of
Procedure and in Annex VII thereto. Before the confirmation hearings, the President of the
Parliament invites the President-elect of the Commission to inform the Conference of Presidents
about (i) the planned structure of the new Commission, including the proposed titles of individual
portfolios and their possible grouping; (ii) the allocation of responsibilities (portfolios) in the
proposed new College of Commissioners in accordance with the political guidelines of the
President-elect; and (iii) other horizontal questions, including gender balance in the new College.
Once the future Commissioners have been nominated, and taking into account the allocation of
portfolios within the College, the President of the Parliament asks the Commissioners-designate to
appear before the appropriate committees or bodies. On a recommendation from the Conference
of Committee Chairs, the Conference of Presidents organises the hearings; the chair and
coordinators of each committee are responsible for the detailed arrangements. Hearings are held in
public and are broadcast live.
Parliament evaluates the general competence, European commitment and personal independence
of the Commissioners-designate. It assesses their knowledge of their prospective portfolio and their
communication skills, and should also take gender balance into consideration. In addition, Parliament
may comment on the portfolio responsibilities of Commissioners-designate.

Examination of declarations of interests
Before the hearings, the Commissioners-designate submit declarations of interests that fully
disclose information relating to their financial interests. Parliament's Legal Affairs Committee (JURI)
examines each Commissioner-designate's declaration, to confirm that there is no conflict of interest
concerning their future portfolio in the Commission.
If the information provided in the declaration is accurate and complete and contains nothing
indicating an actual or potential conflict of interest in connection with the Commissioner-designate's
portfolio, the Chair of the JURI committee sends a letter confirming the absence of any conflict of
interest to the committees responsible for the confirmation hearing. The same notification
procedure applies if the JURI committee finds elements in the declaration of interests which should
be considered in the overall evaluation of the Commissioner-designate.
The JURI committee can request supplementary information, and invite the Commissioner-
designate to a discussion if information related to financial interests in the declaration of interests
is incomplete or contradictory, or if there is a need for further information regarding financial
interests. However, such a discussion is not a hearing, which is held later on.
If the JURI committee identifies a conflict of interest related to financial interests based on the
declaration of interests or the supplementary information supplied by the Commissioner-designate,
it draws up recommendations aimed at resolving the conflict of interest. In the most serious cases,
as a last resort the JURI committee may conclude that the Commissioner-designate is unable to
exercise their functions in accordance with the Treaties and the Code of Conduct. In that case, the
Rules of Procedure (Article 3 para 3 (c) Annex VII) provide that the President of the Parliament
informs the President-elect of the Commission, who would decide on what steps to take.
Although the consequences of a negative evaluation of a Commissioner-designate's declaration of
interests are not explicitly specified, Annex VII of the Rules of Procedure provides for the suspension
of the appointment procedure for the Commissioner-designate concerned until a solution to the
conflict of interest is found or the President-elect decides on what steps to take. However, if the
President-elect of the Commission does not follow the recommendation of the negative opinion, it
could put at risk the approval of the whole Commission by Parliament.
Parliament has also attempted to reinforce scrutiny over the Commission by extending the scope of
the declarations of interests, as they were considered too narrow and ambiguous. Following



                                                                                                     5
                                                    138
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Parliament's calls to modify the 2011 Code of Conduct for Commissioners, the 2018 Code of Conduct
broadened the scope of the declaration of interests that members of the Commission have to
present to include, among other things, all financial interests worth more than €10 000 (regardless
of whether they are likely to constitute a conflict of interest); the financial interests of minor children,
where those might be considered capable of giving rise to a conflict of interest; and membership of
associations, political parties, trade unions, NGOs or other bodies if their activities are intended to
influence or affect the exercise of public functions.
Parliament examines the declarations in the context of the hearings of Commissioners-designate
before their appointment. The declarations are updated at least annually, scrutinised under the
authority of the President of the Commission, then published. In addition, the Code of Conduct
requires information to be published on meetings between Commissioners and interest
representatives, the costs of Commissioners' individual business travel, and a list of gifts
Commissioners receive in their official capacity.
Since 2021, Parliament has pushed for the creation of an independent ethics body that would, in
addition to proposing and advising on ethics standards across the EU institutions, assist the JURI
committee in the assessment of possible conflicts of interest of Commissioners-designate prior to
hearings. Ultimately, in 2024, the Parliament approved an agreement between eight EU institutions
and bodies (not including the European Council) to create a new Body for Ethical Standards. This
Body will develop, update, and interpret common minimum standards for ethical conduct, and will
publish reports on how these standards have been reflected in each signatory's internal rules. The
institutions participating in the Body will be represented by one senior member and the position of
Chair of the Body will rotate every year between the institutions. Five independent experts will
support its work and be available for consultation by the participating institutions and bodies about
standardised written declarations, including declarations of interest. The interinstitutional
agreement specified that the declarations of financial interests of the Commissioners-designate
should, as a rule, be subject to examination by the independent experts' agreement.

Organisation of the hearings: Committee responsible and allocation of time
Once the JURI committee examines the declarations of Commissioners-designate, the confirmation
hearings take place before the appropriate parliamentary committees or bodies. On the
recommendation of the Conference of Committee Chairs, the Conference of Presidents decides on
the division of competences for the confirmation hearings. There is one hearing per Commissioner-
designate, generally organised in Brussels (occasionally hearings have been held in Strasbourg).
If the portfolio of a Commissioner-designate falls within the remit of one parliamentary committee,
or several committees but with the competence of one committee prevailing, only that committee
will be involved in the hearing (committee responsible). Hearings may, however, involve more than
one parliamentary committee. If a Commissioner-designate's portfolio falls equally within the remit
of more than one committee, they shall be heard jointly by those committees (joint committee).
Other committees may be invited to participate in the confirmation hearing if the portfolio falls to a
substantial extent within their responsibility.
The Commissioners' portfolios often fall across the remits of different parliamentary committees; in
the 2014 hearings, there were nine hearings with joint committees and nine with associated
committees. For Commissioners-designate (or Vice-Presidents) with cross-cutting competences, a
different format may be used provided that the committee responsible is involved. As it is
impossible to involve all committees in individual hearings, even if the portfolio touches on their
remit, Parliament proposed a different format following the 2014 experience: a meeting of the
Conference of Presidents or of the Conference of Committee Chairs open to members of the
committees responsible. In 2014, a hearing before the Conference of Presidents open to all Members
was the format used for the First Vice-President, Frans Timmermans, whose competences had a
distinctly horizontal nature.




6
                                                 139
                                                  Confirmation hearings of the Commissioners-designate


Committees submit written questions to the Commissioners-designate before the hearings, with
Commissioners-designate receiving two common questions drafted by the Conference of
Committee Chairs. The first question is on their general competence, European commitment and
personal independence. The second one is on the management of the portfolio and their cooperation
with Parliament. The committee responsible for the hearing drafts five policy-specific questions
about their main priorities and legislative initiatives. In the case of joint committees, each may ask
three questions; each invited committee shall have the right to submit one question; sub-questions
are not allowed. The curriculum vitae of the Commissioners-designate and their answers to the
written questions are made available on Parliament's website before the hearings.
Each hearing lasts three hours in principle. Exceptionally, in the case of Commissioners-designate
with particularly large or complex portfolios involving more than one committee, the Conference of
Committee Chairs may recommend that the confirmation hearing be scheduled to last for up to four
hours. The recommended length of the confirmation hearing should take due account of the number
of invited committees in order to allow each of their Chairs to ask one question.
Commissioners-designate make an opening statement of no more than 15 minutes and a session of
questions and answers follows. Speaking time is allocated to political groups taking into account the
size of each group (including the non-attached Members), as required under Rule 178. The speaking
time allocated to a political group is treated as a block, subject to the need to maintain a single
harmonised format for each individual confirmation hearing. It is up to the political group to
distribute that time between its Members participating in the hearing; speaking time allocated to
non-attached Members is not to be treated as a block. The Commissioner-designate should be
given, on average, twice as much time for their reply as the time given for the question.
The confirmation hearings aim to develop a pluralistic political dialogue between the
Commissioners-designate and the Members. Before the end of the confirmation hearing, the
Commissioners-designate are given the opportunity to make a brief closing statement. A live
audiovisual transmission of the confirmation hearings is made available free of charge to the public
and a recording is made available within 24 hours.

Evaluation of Commissioners-designate after the hearings
The chair and coordinators of the committee responsible for the hearing (or of the joint committees)
meet in camera without delay after the hearing to evaluate the Commissioner-designate's suitability
to be a member of the Commission and their suitability to deal with the assigned portfolio. There is
a single evaluation letter for each candidate, including the opinions of invited committees. If the
coordinators unanimously approve or reject the Commissioner-designate, a letter of approval or
rejection will be submitted. If coordinators representing a majority of at least two-thirds of the
committee membership approve the candidate's performance, the letter of approval will state that
the candidate was approved by a large majority of the committee and minority views will be
mentioned if requested.
If the two-thirds majority is not attained, the coordinators shall request further information from the
Commissioner-designate through written questions and, if they are still dissatisfied, can request a
resumed hearing of one and a half hours subject to the approval of the Conference of Presidents. If
the coordinators are still unable to reach the two-thirds majority, the chair will convene a committee
meeting and call a vote, first, on the candidate's suitability to become a member of the Commission,
and then on their suitability to carry out the duties assigned. Committees' evaluation letters are to
be transmitted within 24 hours of the completion of the evaluation. These letters are examined by
the Conference of Committee Chairs and conveyed to the Conference of Presidents. The
Conference of Presidents then declares the hearings closed and authorises the publication of all
letters of evaluation, which are then published simultaneously.
After the hearings conclude, the President-elect presents the College of Commissioners and its
programme in plenary; the Presidents of the European Council and of the Council are invited to




                                                                                                     7
                                                 140
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


attend. The statement is followed by a debate, and any political group or at least one twentieth of
the Members of Parliament ('low threshold') may table a motion for a resolution. Finally, consent to
the Commission as a whole is given by a vote in plenary, requiring a majority of votes cast, by roll
call. The new Commission is then formally appointed by the European Council, acting by qualified
majority.

Critical issues in past hearings
The first Commission subjected to hearings was the Santer Commission in 1995, where the
parliamentary committees made many critical comments on certain Commissioners-designate, and
asked for Irishman Padraig Flynn to be assigned a different portfolio. The main point of criticism in
the hearings was the lack of specialist knowledge of some candidates about their portfolio, as well
as their vague answers and reluctance to make commitments, invoking the collegiate decision-
making of the Commission. This led in subsequent hearings to Commissioners-designate preparing
more thoroughly on the concrete policy fields and even making firm commitments and signing
commitments proposed by a parliamentary committee – a practice established by the Economic and
Monetary Affairs Committee in 2010, with Joaquín Almunia, Michel Barnier, and Olli Rehn.
Parliament pressed for the first time for the replacement of a Commissioner-designate in 2004.
Among other things, the Italian nominee Rocco Buttiglione drew sharp criticism for his comments
on the role of women and on homosexuality. President-elect José Manuel Barroso was obliged to
ask Parliament to postpone its vote, and came back with two new Commissioners-designate and a
change in another portfolio. Buttiglione's appointment was withdrawn by the Italian government,
and Franco Frattini was nominated, inheriting the Justice, Freedom and Security portfolio from his
unsuccessful predecessor. Latvia also withdrew Ingrida Udre's nomination, owing to her possible
involvement in political and legal malpractice; Andris Piebalgs was nominated instead. Hungarian
nominee László Kovács, who had been criticised for his lack of preparation and specific knowledge
of his future portfolio (Energy) was assigned a different portfolio (Taxation and Customs Union). 9
In 2009, the focus fell on Bulgarian nominee Rumiana Jeleva, whose business dealings and her
competence in her portfolio were questioned in the hearing. As a consequence, the Bulgarian
government withdrew her nomination and proposed Kristalina Georgieva instead.
In 2014, Slovenian nominee Alenka Bratušek, a former prime minister, was rejected by the
Committees on Environment, Public Health and Food Safety, and Industry, Research and Energy as
the Vice-President for Energy Union due to her poor knowledge of the portfolio and ethical concerns
linked to her appointment as Commissioner-designate by a government headed by herself. Slovenia
withdrew her nomination and appointed Violeta Bulc, who took up her duties as Commissioner for
Transport. Hungarian nominee Tibor Navracsics did receive approval as Commissioner-designate,
but not for the proposed portfolio (Commissioner for Education, Youth, Culture and Citizenship),
with MEPs criticising the attribution of competences related to citizenship due to his close ties with
the Hungarian prime minister. President-elect Juncker changed his initial portfolio to secure
Parliament's approval.
In 2019, Parliament demonstrated its growing power in scrutinising the composition of the future
Commission. For the first time, the JURI committee did not approve the declarations of financial
interests for the Commissioner-designate for Transport, Rovana Plumb (Romania), and the
Commissioner-designate for Neighbourhood and Enlargement, László Trócsányi (Hungary). Even
after personal appearances, the JURI committee did not approve their general declarations of
interests, which effectively barred them from taking any position in the College. Such a statement,
due to its political consequences, blocked their participation in the confirmation hearings before the
designated parliamentary committees. Ultimately, the unsuccessful candidates were replaced by
Adina Vălean (Romania) and Olivér Várhelyi (Hungary), who became the Commissioners for the
respective portfolios.




8
                                                141
                                                 Confirmation hearings of the Commissioners-designate


Additionally, in 2019, the Committee for Internal Market and Consumer Protection and the
Committee for Industry, Research and Energy rejected Sylvie Goulard, the French pick as
Commissioner for Internal Market, due to various allegations connected to her previous political
work. France replaced her with Thierry Breton, who was eventually confirmed as Commissioner.
Recent controversies have revealed a highly politicised appointment process. The hearings revealed
Parliament's difficulties in examining Commissioners-designate thoroughly, owing to a lack of time
and detailed documentation about candidates. Parliament declared that the 2019 format for
hearings, including the examination of the declarations of interests, was an important step in
increasing the Commission's accountability to Parliament, but also admitted the procedure could be
further improved.

Strengthening the Commission's democratic accountability
The hearings of Commissioners-designate have helped to increase the political dynamic in relations
between Parliament and the Commission, similar to that found in some national contexts. Many
experts and stakeholders credit the procedure with having further politicised the European
Commission, 10 and therefore also the EU decision-making process, which is seen by many as a
necessary step towards further EU democratisation. Similarly, it is widely agreed that the hearings
play a major role in the Commission's accountability to Parliament. While seen as a confrontation,
once passed it strengthens the new Commissioners' positions. This is all the more important given
Parliament's increasing efforts to shape, together with the Commission and Council, the Union's
annual and multiannual programming (Article 17(1) TEU). Hearings could therefore become the
starting point for stronger political agenda-setting at EU level.
However, the growing politicisation of the confirmation hearings is not universally welcomed. Some
point out that a parliamentary-based Commission would not be independent enough and would not
have the legitimacy to carry out its traditional duties – for example, of acting as guardian of the
Treaties or monopolising the right of legislative initiative. 11 Criticism has been expressed by some
experts that Commissioners-designate are tested in the confirmation hearings on their competence
in the portfolio assigned, whereas the Treaties stipulate that the Commissioners should be chosen
on the basis of their 'general competence and European commitment'.
Moreover, the focus on the political views of individual Commissioners-designate is seen by some
as contradictory to the Parliament being able only to approve or reject the Commission as a whole.
The possibility of Parliament rejecting the whole College because of the bad performance of one
Commissioner-designate is thus seen as a disproportionate measure.12 Some argue that MEPs' far
more aggressive interrogation of candidates from opposing European political families than of those
belonging to their own results in a power struggle, not only within the institutional triangle of the
Parliament, Council and Commission, but also between the political groups in Parliament. This
approach might also reduce the ability to build stable majorities throughout the legislative period.

BRIEFING
European Council Briefing



            Setting the European political
               priorities for 2024-2029
The EU's priorities for the 5-year institutional cycle are outlined in the European Council's Strategic
Agenda. Subsequently, the European Commission sets its priorities in the president's political
guidelines, which are a first step in operationalising the EU priorities outlined in the Strategic
Agenda. These priorities will then be translated into concrete initiatives included in the
Commission's annual work programmes, before being submitted to the European Parliament and
the Council of the EU in the form of legislative (and non-legislative) proposals.
This briefing outlines the main policy priorities for the EU in the coming years, and analyses the
differences in views of the European Council and the European Commission. With some exceptions,
the European Council's and the Commission's policy priorities generally converge, as both
documents, although organised differently, focus on three main priorities: democracy, security and
competitiveness. When comparing them with the political priorities of the previous institutional
cycle, it becomes evident that democracy and defence have gained in importance, while climate and
energy are less salient than before. The analysis shows that the political guidelines pay significantly
more attention to social issues than the Strategic Agenda. Conversely, the two policy clusters
'external policies' and 'climate and energy' receive more attention in the Strategic Agenda. However,
external policy in general now feeds into all policy areas, intertwining with internal policies. While
this is more explicit in the Strategic Agenda, the emphasis on Europe's role in the word runs like a
red thread through both documents, reflecting a major shift away from past political priorities.
The comparison identifies the topics that are missing from one or the other document, points out
where different approaches are being taken on specific policy issues and where particular concepts
have changed since the last institutional cycle. The briefing also outlines the specific initiatives
mentioned in the political guidelines by policy cluster, notably those with a timeline.
The EU's political priorities in two strategic documents
The EU's priorities for the 5-year institutional cycle are a combination of the European Council's
Strategic Agenda, reflecting the views of Member States and the political balance within the
institution, and the political guidelines drafted by the candidate for European Commission
President, which take the results of the European Parliament elections, and the corresponding
weight of the political families, into consideration. Both documents influence Council Presidency
programmes, Commission annual work programmes, and legislative proposals put forward by the
Commission – and, in turn, the legislative work of Parliament and the Council.
While both documents set out the political priorities for the EU, the nature of each differs. The
Strategic Agenda sets strategic orientations for the EU at the macro level, without indicating specific
initiatives or a specific timeline. Moreover, it needs to be adopted by all EU Heads of State or
Government by unanimity, meaning the content cannot be too controversial and often leaves some
room for interpretation. The political guidelines, while also mentioning the macro objectives, in
addition indicate specific initiatives, some even with a concrete timeline. This difference in nature is
also reflected in the documents' respective length, whereby the Strategic Agenda is rather short and
the political guidelines tend to be much longer.

                          EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service
                              Author: Ralf Drachenberg; Graphics: Samy Chahri
                                      European Council Oversight Unit
                                        PE 762.863 – October 2024                                          EN
                                                 144
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


Strategic Agenda 2024-2029
The European Council is composed of the Heads of State or Government of the EU Member States,
as well as its President and the President of the European Commission. Its role, as defined in
Article 15(1) of the Treaty on European Union (TEU), is to 'provide the Union with the necessary
impetus for its development' and to define its 'general political directions and priorities'.
The Strategic Agenda 2024-2029 was adopted by the European Council on 27 June 2024. As
described in an EPRS briefing, it results from a year-long process to which the Member States – and
EU Heads of State or Government personally – contributed. The outgoing Commission President,
and now President-elect, Ursula von der Leyen, was largely excluded from this process by the
European Council President, Charles Michel, as she was not invited to participate in any of the small
group meetings with Members of the European Council, which took place as part of the preparatory
process.
European Commission political guidelines
In the political guidelines, the candidate for the office of European Commission President outlines
their view of the EU priorities for the next 5 years. This document then forms the basis for the
subsequent design of the Commissioners' portfolios.
Ursula von der Leyen presented her Political Guidelines 2024-2029 on 18 July 2024. The guidelines
are always published shortly after the Strategic Agenda. This timing is designed to enable EU leaders
to influence the Commission's priorities for the next 5 years, which - as specified by
President von der Leyen in the introduction – 'draw on … the European Council's Strategic Agenda
for 2024-2029'., and 'on my consultations and on the common ideas discussed with the democratic
forces in the European Parliament'.
Main political priorities
Structure
The structures of the Strategic Agenda and the political guidelines differ considerably. The Strategic
Agenda is based on three policy priorities and 10 sub-priorities (see Figure 1).

    Figure 1 – Strategic Agenda 2024-2029

                                                  145
                                                    Setting the European political priorities for 2024-2029


Conversely, the political guidelines have six policy priorities and a seventh horizontal priority on
'delivering together and preparing our Union for the future', which includes three sub-themes: the
multiannual financial framework (MFF), reforms and institutional cooperation (see Table 1).

 A free and democratic Europe
                                        competitiveness

 A strong and secure Europe             2. A new era for European defence and security

However, while the political guidelines have seven priorities, von der Leyen stated three core
priorities: 'prosperity', 'security' and 'democracy'. These overlap one to one with the three priorities
outlined in the European Council's Strategic Agenda.
Comparison
An analysis of both the overlap and the differences between the policy priorities in the two
documents shows that:
     there is a strong overlap between the priorities 'A free and democratic Europe' and
      'Protecting our democracy, upholding our values', as well as with some elements from
      'A global Europe: Leveraging our power and partnerships';
     the priority 'A prosperous and competitive Europe' covers most elements of the
      priority 'A new plan for Europe's sustainable prosperity and competitiveness', and
      some aspects of the priority 'Supporting people, strengthening our societies and our
      social model';
     the elements under the Strategic Agenda's priority 'A strong and secure Europe' can
      be found in the political guidelines mainly under the priorities 'A new era for European
      defence and security' and 'A global Europe: Leveraging our power and partnerships';
     social issues addressed in the political guidelines under the priority 'Supporting
      people, strengthening our societies and our social model' have no corresponding
      chapter in the Strategic Agenda and are only briefly addressed in the sub-priority
      'Advancing together'.
An examination of the overlap and differences between horizontal topics yields the following results.
     The political guidelines' chapter 'Delivering together and preparing our Union for the
      future', with its three sub-themes – MFF, reforms and institutional cooperation – has
      no corresponding chapter in the Strategic Agenda.




                                                                                                         3
                                                 146
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


     One of the sub-themes – the next MFF – is addressed at the end of the introduction
      of the Strategic Agenda. Both documents stress the need to focus EU spending better
      on the EU's priorities and to address the need for new own resources.
     The delivery priority's second sub-theme – reforms – is addressed in the Strategic
      Agenda under the sub-priority 'Preparing a bigger and stronger Union' within the
      priority 'A strong and secure Europe'. In the context of enlargement, both documents
      acknowledge the need to address necessary internal reforms in order to ensure that
      EU policies are future-proof. However, while the follow-up to the Conference on the
      Future of Europe and the need for Treaty change are stressed in the political
      guidelines, they are not mentioned in the Strategic Agenda.
     While cooperation with the European Parliament is the third sub-theme in the political
      guidelines' delivery priority, relations between the EU institutions are hardly
      mentioned in the Strategic Agenda. The only reference to interinstitutional relations
      can be found at the end of the introduction, which stresses the need for 'the
      institutional balance of powers as set out in the Treaties and the principles of
      conferral, subsidiarity and proportionality' to be respected.
Changes to the priorities since the last institutional cycle
When comparing the political priorities with those of the 2019-2024 period (for details, see EPRS
analysis), the following points can be identified.
Owing to changes in the geopolitical reality over recent years, the political priorities place stronger
emphasis on the EU's external role, in particular regarding defence and enlargement. External
policy in general now feeds into all policy areas and, in the case of the European Council's Strategic
Agenda, internal and external policy are systematically intertwined. This is a novelty, as external
policy – entitled 'Promoting Europe's interests and values in the world' and 'A stronger Europe in the
world' respectively – has in the past constituted a separate headline priority. This is an important
conceptual change, mainstreaming external relations.
The focus on sovereignty and economic security, which was also stressed in the report by the
former President of the European Central Bank, Mario Draghi, is another addition to the EU's long-
term political priorities. While previously used only in the context of digital policies, the focus on
sovereignty is now used in various strategic sectors, notably defence, energy, food, and economic
policy more broadly. This reflects lessons the EU learned from the COVID-19 pandemic and the war
in Ukraine. A call for sovereignty in these strategic sectors has been mentioned in numerous
European Council conclusions over recent years, and is now used for the first time in the long-term
strategic priorities.
A noteworthy shift compared with the previous institutional cycle is the first-time inclusion of a
specific priority on democracy in the Strategic Agenda, which even tops the list of priorities.
Previous Commission political guidelines already had a priority on democracy, and so do the current
guidelines: von der Leyen states that 'strengthening the rule of law will be our daily work and duty'.
Migration and internal security have a similarly high standing as in the previous institutional cycle's
political priorities. However, the perspective has widened this time around. Already a significant
topic in the 2019-2024 institutional cycle, migration was, however, considered in the Strategic
Agenda primarily from the angle of the fight against irregular migration. By contrast, the previous
political guidelines already referred to the need to set up legal pathways for migration. For the
current period, both documents give broader scope to the migration concept.
Another common feature of both documents is that green growth, climate and energy do not
constitute a headline priority, unlike in the 2019-2024 institutional cycle. Although they are
addressed in connection with different priorities by the European Council and the Commission
President, the mere fact that these topics do not appear as a main priority indicates a significant
shift in policy priorities compared with the previous period. Moreover, when they are referred to in
the documents, the topics are mostly presented from a competitiveness and investment



4
                                                  147
                                                    Setting the European political priorities for 2024-2029


perspective, the two main themes running through the political guidelines. Nevertheless, the
objective of building a 'genuine energy union' has been given greater weight, which is linked to the
changed geopolitical situation and the need to secure energy supplies.
Health has also grown in importance, albeit on a smaller scale, as a result of the COVID-19 pandemic
and the need to ensure preparedness.
Besides the headline priorities, the focus of the European Commission's political guidelines is also
reflected in the motto the institution gives itself. While the 2014-2019 Commission wanted to be
known as a 'political Commission', the 2019-2024 Commission defined itself as a 'geopolitical
Commission'. Now, its President referred to the new Commission as an 'investment Commission' –
'investment' also being the concept most often mentioned in the political guidelines. By contrast,
the concept most often referred to in the European Council's Strategic Agenda is 'security' (the
second-most used concept in the political guidelines).
Furthermore, the meaning of concepts can change from one institutional cycle to another. In the
Political Guidelines 2019-2024, the priority 'Protecting our European way of life' addressed issues
such as the rule of law, strong borders, migration and internal security. In the Political Guidelines
2024-2029, the Commission President refers again to the 'European way of life', but in the context
of the EU's social model as well as its culture and history. Moreover, she states that 'farming is a core
part of our European way life – and it must always stay that way'.
A similar expansion of concept can be observed with resilience and preparedness. In the last
Strategic Agenda, the concept applied primarily to natural and man-made disasters, while in this
institutional cycle, it is given a much broader scope, to include democracy, trade, and a clear defence
and security component.
Policy clusters
As outlined above, the Strategic Agenda and the policy guidelines are structured around priorities
that differ in terms of their number, name, order and the policy issues they include. To be able to
compare the policy content, the analysis in this briefing is therefore organised by policy cluster:
(i) Justice and home affairs; (ii) Economic policies; (iii) Employment and social affairs; (iv) External
policies; and (v) Climate and energy.
General attention to policy clusters
As seen in Figure 2 below, the attention (expressed in word count) paid to each policy cluster varies,
sometimes significantly, between the two documents.
The most striking difference is that social and employment policy received over twice as much
attention in the political guidelines as in the strategic agenda. The lower salience of social and
employment issues in the strategic agenda could be explained by the fact that there were during
the process fewer Heads of State or Government belonging to the family of European socialists in
the European Council, and only four when the document was finalised in June 2024. Conversely, the
political guidelines took into consideration the political forces in the European Parliament, where the
Alliance of Socialist and Democrats is the second biggest group.
Moreover, the two policy clusters 'external policies' and 'climate and energy' receive substantially
less attention in the political guidelines that they do in the strategic agenda.




                                                                                                         5
                                                  148
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service



    Figure 2 – Attention paid to different policy clusters




    Source: Compiled by the author, 2024.


Specific policy differences
As the political guidelines are a longer document than the Strategic Agenda, it could theoretically
mention all the elements outlined in the Strategic Agenda. In practice, it does cover almost
everything, although a number of policy concepts specifically addressed in the Strategic Agenda are
not mentioned in the political guidelines. These include:
       Sustainable Development Goals: the Strategic Agenda calls on the EU to promote the
        achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals, a reference not included in the
        political guidelines;
       human rights at global level: the Strategic Agenda explicitly calls on the EU to
        promote human rights at global level and defend them in international forums, while
        the political guidelines refer solely to the human rights situation inside the EU, in
        particular in the context of asylum procedures;
       entrepreneurship: while the Strategic Agenda calls for 'unleash[ing] the European
        spirit of entrepreneurship', the political guidelines do not mention the concept.
Conversely, some EU policy concepts – asylum, fisheries, and citizens' participation in the
formulation of EU policy recommendations for improved democratic legitimacy – are detailed in the
political guidelines, but not mentioned in the Strategic Agenda.
Sometimes, the same topic is addressed in both documents, but the approach taken differs slightly.
       Foreign direct investment is viewed differently in the Strategic Agenda and the
        political guidelines. While the former stresses the need to attract foreign investment,
        the latter cautions about the potential risks of foreign direct investment.
       State aid: the Strategic Agenda calls for a 'balanced and effective State aid and
        competition framework'; the political guidelines mention the revision of State aid rules
        specifically in the context of enabling housing support.
       International institutions: the Strategic Agenda calls for upholding the United Nations
        (UN) and the principles enshrined in the UN Charter. The political guidelines mention



6
                                                   149
                                                     Setting the European political priorities for 2024-2029


        the UN in the context of a reform of the international system. Both documents also
        stress the World Trade Organization's central role, with the political guidelines calling
        for reforming that organisation, as well.
