Initiatives with a timeline
Some of the initiatives outlined are already linked with a specific time horizon, namely the
commitment to be put forward within the first 100 days. These are:
       clean industrial deal for competitive industries and quality jobs;
       European action plan on the cybersecurity of hospitals and healthcare providers;
       AI Factories initiative;
       white paper on the future of European defence;
       first annual youth policy dialogues;
       a vision for agriculture and food;
       pre-enlargement policy reviews.
Other specific initiatives to be proposed soon after, in 2025, include:
       a new European biotech act;
       first set of new important projects of common European interest (IPCEIs);
       new pact for European social dialogue;
       a new long-term budget.
Conclusion
The EU's political priorities, updated every 5 years at the beginning of each new institutional cycle,
are expressed in the European Council's Strategic Agenda and the European Commission's political
guidelines. The analysis of these strategic documents has shown a general convergence of the
European Council's and the Commission's policy priorities; both documents, although organised
differently, focus on three main priorities: democracy, security and competitiveness. When looking
at specific policy clusters, the analysis shows that the political guidelines pay significantly more
attention to social issues than the Strategic Agenda. Conversely, the two policy clusters of 'external
policies' and 'climate and energy' receive more attention in the Strategic Agenda. Sometimes, the
same topic is addressed in both documents, but with a slightly differing approach, notably regarding
foreign direct investment and State aid.



                                                                                                          7
                                                   150
EPRS | European Parliamentary Research Service


A comparison with the political priorities of the previous institutional cycle shows some important
developments. Taking the experience from recent crises and the changing geopolitical environment
into account, external relations are given a prominent role. Moreover, in the case of the European
Council's Strategic Agenda, relations with third countries and partners are mainstreamed
throughout all EU policy areas. This new way of perceiving the EU's relations with the rest of the
world highlights the fact that external relations are not a separate matter, but intertwined with EU
policies and impacting on them. Moreover, democracy as well as defence have gained in importance
in the new institutional cycle, while climate and energy are less salient than before.
The EU's political priorities, as outlined in the Strategic Agenda and the political guidelines, are likely
to be reflected in subsequent legislative and non-legislative proposals put forward by the
Commission, and consequently, in the European Parliament's and the Council of the EU's day-to-
day work. The political guidelines, having a stronger operational dimension than the Strategic
Agenda, already include many forthcoming initiatives, some associated with a specific timeline.
Beyond the priorities included in the Strategic Agenda and agreed by the EU Member States by
consensus, some differences of views on the means of implementation have already emerged. One
such example could be the need to invest more in Europe's economy, for the continent to stay
competitive, a priority also outlined in the Draghi report. While this requirement is generally not
disputed, the exact way of putting it into practice is highly debated. There are a number of other
topics on which differences in national positions suggest that challenging discussions in the new
term are likely; these include, in particular, migration, the MFF, and relations with third-country
economic partners and rivals.
While the general political priorities for this institutional cycle are now set, the European Council will
continue to fine-tune and adjust them, if this is necessary in reaction to internal and international
developments, by issuing conclusions on relevant topics during its meetings. The scrutiny of and
debate on these activities in the European Parliament are essential.
